LADIES' LETTER.

MY DRAB. Nras.zz,

The increasing price of furs obliges many to forego them altogether because really good fur commands now a very high price. There is an increasing predilection for taffetas or gauze ruffles, some of which are plain, whilst others are just hemmed with fur. Some of these ruffles are trimmed with brown or grey marabout, which gives a light and fluffy effect; the muff must, of course, be en suite. New and dainty muffs are formed, frills and all, of closely-set violets, which look like gauged velvet at a little distance, with a big bow of purple velvet, fixed with the green leaves, in the centre.

Fur is applied to every possible garment in season, ermine being the favourite of the hour. Tea-gowns and matinee gowns are lined with it, and even white silk or satin petticoats, to within a few inches of the waist, whence stcckingette replacn it, to prevent thickness round the hips. Ermine is also applied to many theatre blouses with good results, especially when worn with a black tulle or chiffon toque with white aigrette. A charming example had the yoke, top part of the sleeves, pinafore-front, and back of the blouse in ermine, whilst at the sides peeped through nzi under-bodice of cream woollen guipure, picked out with silver paillettes, mounted over ivory satin ; the puffed gauntlets to the wrist were to match, whilst the collar-band of guipure was con- tinued into a jabot of lace, terminating in a knot at the left of the yoke, together with a large _rick pink rose.

dinner gown is of black silk witl of pale blue velvet inserted and partly hidden by a herringbone stitching of black silk. Very fine tucks intervene between the bands of velvet on -both bodice and skirt, the latter being fitted to the figure with narrow tucks below the waist. The band below the yoke of jet and steel embroidery is fined with blue velvet, and also the one on the skirt that heads the tucked and frilled and lace ,trimmed silk flounce. The yoke and under. sleeves are in embroidered net. The sleeves them- sselves are in jet and steel embroidery over tucked black silk. A very graceful evening gown is made .of cri'-pe•de-chine in the brightest tone of pale reddish mauve, the skirt pleated and trimmed with graduated band of creamy lace insertion. A fichu in jewelled lace is folded on the shoulders and the sleeves are finished with bands of Qinailar lace.

A pretty fancy is the bolero of flowers tor even- ing wear, formed of a light tracery or network of flowers, small flowers, such as miniature roses or violets, for preference, edged with a ruching of leaves. It demands a light airy fabric, such °as tulle or mousseline, for its proper complement, and a slim and youthful wearer : than the effect is charming. Some evening boleros are composed of laee or chiffon, covered with a floral applique in painted mousseliue or crepe flowers, raised and padded in relief, and united by stems and leaves in floss silk or chenille. These fairy-like boleros are in great demand for evening wear, but they are all of the lightest and most transparent character, and those of embroidery, lace, and applique are in far better style than the tasteless heavy sequined and jetted ones. Children's fashions are not liable to the storm

and stress that agitate their elders' toillettes. For party occasions this year, just as last, soft silk with lace insertions for wee girls and riflg. spotted net for lassies over 10 or 11 are ordered. To show little white silk mittens on soft pink hands and arms, sleeves are cut sometimes to three. quarter length. For a dhildren's fancy-dress dance is, moreover, a comparathaly easy task to contrive a costume which shall be, net only effec- tive, but inexpensive; at any rate, so far as girls are concerned. Boys' fancy dresses, it must be admitted, present rather more difficulties. Flower dresses are, perhaps, the easiest of all to manage, and, although somewhat hackneyed, they are always pretty. For the frock is made with a short. frilled skirt of gauze or soft muslin of the colour of the flower chosen, a corselet of pale green velvet or satin trimmed with flowers, and a gauze chemisette and short sleeves. On the head may be worn either a quaint little cap in the shape of a gigantic blossom, or a picturesque hat wreathed with flowers. This general idea may be varied in detail.

Your loving, JENNIE.

To CLEAN MARBLE WASTISTANDS.—Take two of washing soda, one of powdered pumice-stone, and one of powdered chalk. Pound together, and then pass through a sieve. Take some of the powder and make it into a paste with cold water, rub it well over the surface of the marble, and, when all stains are removed, wash off with soap snd water.

WOMEN'S CHAI.

AB persons who are expected to render certain duties 1 or services at the Coronation, are warned in the King's I Proclamation that they must not at their peril, tail to attend, unless they obtain special leave of absence. No doubt in medireval times, their estates would have been 11 forfeit(d, but the present warning seems a little super- fluotia, to judge by the anxiety of all sorts of people to , fulfil some of the other ancient offices, in connection with the " Said Solemnity." Undeterred by the decision of the Court of Claims, ' not to allow appeals,w hen there was no duty to perform, many have been made for such offices as have gone out of date, as that of armour-bearer, bow-bearer, canopy- bearers, and so en. Amongst other appeals that have failed is that of Miss Beatrice Fellows for the privilege of hereditary " herb-etrewer." A former Miss Fellowea, attended the Coronation of George IV, to distribute rosemarY and sweet smelling herbs, in his path but un- fortunately for the present Miss Fellowes this ceremony is to be dispensed with. Several prominent French dressmakers will shortly open premises in London, with a view to taking advan- tage of the brilliant season next year. The coming Coronation will certainly be good for trade, and though in many cases there ray be some doubt about the origin of tho materials, all the peeresses' robes w,ll be made by home establishments, the average cost ranging from £5O to "80. The jewellers also ( xpect to reap a harvest next year, especia'ly now that Society is taking to %searing elaborate jewellery by day, as well as by night. The furnishing and decorating trades whi also greatly benefit by the revival of abrilliane eelain at Court, and the many and costly entettainments which this also means in Society circles. Not only the wealthy firms, but the smaller trades- people are expecting a busy season next year. All the drapera are placing orders for new patterns of Corona- tion goods, and in many towns, Coronation Clubs have been started to provide their members with a new dress or a new suit of clothes, in honour of the great event next June. In former times there appears to have been a general desire on the part of the public, to appear in new garmerataon such occasions, as in Queen Eliza beth's reign, orders were issued in order to check the extravagance of the people. Thus women were for- bidden to wear " monstrous hats in garish colours,"and another regulation forbade any but the nobility to wear woollen stuffs that were manufactured on the continent No such distinction is made nowadays. but it is curi- ous as showing that even in the old times, the nobility had a strange desire to go abroad for their gowns, and finery. A etorm in a teacup has been raging in Paris, over the question whether the term "Madame " should not be applied to unmarried girls. In France, the smallest boy is addressed as " Monsieur," and the Women's Suffrage Society has therefore decided that the title "Mademoiselle" should henceforth he abolished, the idea being that it conveys a suggestion of inferiority with regard to the fair sex. There does not s.lem to be any relevancy in such an argument, but there is, never- theless, a good deal to be said for the adoption of the universal " Madame." It would obviate those mistake. which we are al! familiar with, when in doubt abont addressing women personally, and in correspondence at Mrs or Miss, and this difficulty has been so far ecog- nired in America, that, in many of the cities the car attendants are instructed to address all women and girls as " Madam." Another curious agitation has arisen in France over the new French postage stamps, which display an allegorical figure, holding a tablet, bearing the words "The Rights of Man." This has so offended a certain section of the supporters of " women's rights," that one of their number, Madame Auclert, has had a num- ber of labels printed resembling the Government stamps but bearing the words, "Tie Rights of Women." These labels are to be affixed to all letters, side by side, with the official stamp, as a protest against the invidious distinction it suggests. A similar plan, though for different reasons, was adopted some time ago, lay our own Legitimists or Jacobites, bus in their case they turned the Queen's head upside down, and no doubt the same ingenuity will be displayed by the in-called exnenents of "women's rights'" in'France. Up-te-date hats are high in front and low over the hair at the hack, and show, between these broad limits, the most bewitching curves and angles.—ln these days of ready-made " mounts," the trimming of a bat is not a very difficult feat to accomplish, but with the appear- ance of very moderately priced trimmed headgear, few women trouble to undertake it. There are two or three shops in the West End of London now,which provide an excellent selection of hats and tcquee at Si each. These arrive from Paris evezy day, and though the materials are not of course expensive, they are in the best of taste and style, and are thrown together with the greatest skill. These shops are doing an immense busi- ness, and are patronised by quite smart society women. It is, as they say, quite easy to have added, a feather or other ornament one may have by one, and such head- gear lasts quite as long as is desirable in these days of variety and change. The short walking skirt is now being worn by all really modish individuals, but it is to he feared that some time will elapse befere the generality of women will Nelsen:, to deprive themselves of outdoor " tails," which they are patronising so extensively. Unquestion- ably much illness is brought about by the lengthy out- door skirt, ( specially during wet weather. Getting thoroughly damp about the nem, it .conveys this mois- ture to the petticoats, hose, and footgear, and a severe cold, if nothing worse, is fraquently the result. The home dress m iker will cm ov II to bear in mind the fact that much of the secret of the success of the short , skirt lies in the length. The unskilled worker takes refuge in a compromise making the skirt neither short or long ; or still worse, short in front, but resting a couple of inches on the ground at the back. This gives a hopelessly ungraceful lino. The proper length clears the ground by two inches all round, Some of the newest skirts are being built (with a seam down the front. The mode has little to recommend it, :and will prsbat I 7 not last long. The bleuee is as much in favour as ever, among smart these ere, indeed it is now extremely doubtf. 1 if women. will ever consent te) part with so useful, and altogether desirable garment. Chiffon continues to be the staple material of the evening variety, while silk and lace are employed extensively for afternoon, and outdoor blouses. A perforated washleather bodice is usually worn with the latter, the coatee can then be thrown open when desired, without fear of a chill. Shirts for morning, are generally in flannel, and the naajsrity are cut without any yoke in frost, the lines being carried up to the shoulders. Blouse and skirt, it should be re- membered, must be of precisely the same shade, no contrasts are permissible. Mr Douglas Haw's " Noble Women of our Time," (Isbister and Co., 35 8d) contains biographies of twelve women ,including Miss Daniell—founder if the Soldiers' Homes at Aldershot ; Mrs Sidney Lear,author of " Tales OE a London Parish ;" Mrs Ward, of Withington ; Augusta Countess of Dartmouth, snd the Hon. Mrs Stuart Wortley. It is a bank that should be placed in the hands of all growing girls, but old and young alike , will read and read again, with the keenest interest, and admiration ; the lives of these noble women. MADGE.

WAHRINGTON SAVINGS BANK.

A Successful Year.

Further progress in connection with the Warrington Savings Bank was reported at the annual meeting of the institution held on Tuesday morning. Mr William Long presided, and there were also present the Revs. Canon Willis,Father Whittle, J Yonge, J F Matthews, and T Bishop,Alderman Roberts and Mews JGeddes,T Birchen, John Dowhurst, C Jackson Holmes. J Harrison, G Dakin, T Whittle, RJ Chorlev, and T Tunstall (actuary).

itEPIIIT OF TIIE COMMITTEE OF MANAGEMENT.

The report of this Committee was as follows :—" In presenting the eeneral statement of aecounts of the bank for the year ended 20th November, 1901, the Committee of Management hive much pleasure in drawing atten- tion to the very satisfactory progress of the business of the bank. Seven hundred and seventy-eight new and renewed accounts have ben opened during the year. making tho to at number of depositors 12,891. Twenty- five thousand two hundred and twenteesight transac- tions were recorded as cempared with 24,902 last year . showing an increase of 326. Owing to the ershacned value of money, due to a great extent to the war in South Africa, some of toe larger deposits have been transferred to investments yielding a higher sate of interest than that allowed by the bank, and in conse- quence, the amount due to depositors has decreased by £1,139 171 4i. That mbst of the depesitors prefer abso- lute security rattier than a temporary increase of interest is demonstrated by the facts that theemithdrawals have not been more numerous and that the number of deposi- tors keeps steadily increasing. The amount now due to depositors is £352,323 6s 3d, in addition to which there are investments on their behalf to the extent of £13,720 in stocks. The assets of the hank are £353,402 19s 6i, showieg a surplus of £6,079 13s 3d, after deducting the whole amount due to depositors. The National Debt Commissioners hold on behalf of the inetitution a sum of £353, 651 16s, which is considerably in excess of the total liabilities. The catracted list of depositors' bal- ances, certified by the auditor, is placed on the counter during the hours of business, and any depositor may satisfy himself by its inspection that the amount to his credit, in accordance with his paes-book, is certified to the Government. The bank is open daely for the trans- action of business,'and sums as low so ore shilling are received. Fifty pounds may be deposited in any one year until the amount to the credit of the depositor reaches £2OO. A further £2OO may also be received in any year tor investigation in Government stock. Pay- ments not exceeding £lO are made on demand, larger amounts sv. c paid subject to a week's notice. The con- tinued steady increase in the number of depositors' accounts and in the number of transactions is a most gratifying feature of the bank. \A QUEEN'S SOLDIEM

By WALTER WOOD,

Author of "Barrack and Battlefield," "Famous British Warships," "Through Battle to Pro- motion," "A. Captain in the Fusiliers," "A Soldier of the Legion," &c.

CHAPTER XXI. A IrOPEaTI. PATIENT. Lizzie, ordinarily calm, collected, and essentially resourceful, as befitted her birth and upbringing as a Yorkshire winnan, was, after her short interview with Donald, almost in despair. Without reason, and in spite of the conviction of the doctors, she • had persuaded herself that Mark cupid be brought round to health and strength again without being woefully disfigured for life. She had seen scores of men pass through the surgeons' hands and speedily turn the corner between life and death, and had seen in the operators' fearful skill and work the means of bodily salvation. So familiar had these tasks become that they had almost lost their terrors for her ; beryline that the patient was her own lover, the man who was ,to be her husband, she looked upon the matter with a strangely different ' mind.. From her conchlct she might have been some courageous animal defending her crippled mate from the attacks of watchful, over-mastering enemies. Even Donald, trusted and confided in of old, she looked at with suspicion, uncertain whether he would not at any time come down and exercise his power. In that case Lizzie could do nothing but succumb, and bear her bitter- ness and grief as best she could. By this time she had seen a good deal of the surgeon, and knew that nothing would tern ,him from the strict pers formance of what he looked upon as a professional duty. Mark did not awake from his slumber until he had almost slept the clock round; then he felt, as he truly said, like a new man. He even declared that he was fit to get up and walk and rejoin his corps, but an effort to stand upright shoved him how serious his misapprehension was. "You mustn't do that," said Lizzie, sternk "You, must just rest and keep still." "Or they'll get me even yet,' eli ? " he said, in high spirits. "Well, if 1 can't get up and about • to-day or to-morrow, I will in a week or so, mark my words. Ybe see, when a chap's been brought up on beef and beer and fresh air, 'so to speak, like me, tt isn't as if he'd been dragged out of a ditch or alley,' and had nil flesh and blond to fall back upon. Has that Scotch chap said anything While I've been asleep ?" • ' "He's been very busy," said Lizzie, evasively. "He's like the rest, run off his legs." "Lucky man to have his leis in such good fettle," answered Mark. "I wish you could say the same of mine. Do you know, Liz, I've got a strong idea that I shall never be as hardy on my pins again as I have been ? I feel- that there'll always be stiffness enough to make me hobble. I shall hate that; but I suppo the old man will is have a sort of glum pleasure i it. You know how he used to sneer at the 'No dies ? Well, thank God, if I do bave a hop or a imp for the rest of my days, it'll shew that, I did something else than . just piny at soldiers. Weve shewn 'em that, any- way, the Yeomen are just as good, as anybody else." , "Nobodj, ever said they were not," answered Lizzie, quickly. "They, couldn't.', "Didn't they just?" asked Mark. "Don't you know yet that it was because FO many people said so that I came out here ? I've liked the experience : and I've liked the work, !Ind so on.; but, all the saute, I was just Ineglied And tauntedinto it. The old man's sharp tongue put the finishing touch to the busieess." "Well, it's (long with now," said Lizzie, in tones of thankfulness. "Whatever happens, you'll be no more good for the war." . "We shall seer" said Mark. "Tm not going to skulk and malinger here, you know, Liz. As soon as they fix'ine up and make rue fit again,l'm going back to the front if they want me. It's' my duty, you see, and I can't be kept from it by a bit of a bullet wound. Alter all, when you consider what other men have gone through, mine's only a bit of a hurt." "Don't you talk so glibly about a bit of a hurt,,"' snapped Lizzie. "It's a lot worse than you think, and quite in bad as any other man's had in the same place." . • A , I " Milt° ! " said Mark, in surprise. "What's the trouble ? You sound from your voice as if you ' wanted to n.ake an elephant out of a mouse." "I want to make. it just what it is and no more, and I tell you it's bad enough to keep you from going beck to the front. I know that," said Lizzie, in such uncompromising tones that the patient gazed and looked at her without replying. : One thing was now very clear, that if Lizzie could prevent it he would not . return to the firing line. It was perhaps wrong of her to adopt this : attitude, bet site did not pretend to be more than a woman first and a nurse afterwards. If she had ' misgivings at all, she overcame them by remembering that. her chief purpose in coming out to the war nag to look after Mark if site should get the chance. ' After-a pause. Mark said: "What's the next part of the programme, Liz ?" "You'll he invalided home," she answered. "There's a big batch going very soon, and I fancy you'll go with it." • "Yes; that is, if I don't get well; but---" "There's no but about it, you won't: Oughtn't I to know?" again snapped Lizzie, who had -suddenly become very unamiable. "Well, I suppose so, it's your trade," agreed Mark, "and every man to his trade. Well, what then ? " "You'll go back to the coast and sail by one of the transports .or hospital ships ; then you.'ll go into hospital in England till you're all right. After that they'll send you home. You see, it isn't as if you were a real soldier." Mark sprang up in bed. "By George, Li!" he said. "That's the nastiest cut of all! You've rubbed it in worse than all the rest." "Rubbed what in? " site demanded. "Why, that about the make-believe of it," grienbled Mark. "Oh, bother it!" she said: "Get that silly nonsense out of your head. If people say it they don't mean it. Besides, even it has been make- believe by you, hasn't it been the same with some other people in this war ? Haven't some of_ the generals and other officers been doing the same ' thin"? %%Iv, even some of the nurses have." T.izzie smiled rather bitterly. She,,was thinking of Lad' Mildred. "I don't care about either,the officers or anybody else," said Mark, stubbornly, "so far as that goes. I've only got to consider myself and put myself right. I tell you the whole of this business, so far as we're concerned, sprang up jest because of idle mueslis like yours, nothing else. So you see how necessary it is to put the muzzle on sometimes." "Well," said Lizzie, pacifically, "you must con- sider it unsaid. I snppose you don't want me to think yo ere sorry you came ?" "I am. and lain not," answered Mark. "I fancy I'm a bit queer in my way of looking at this business ; but I haven't had the sport out of it that some of its have got. I suppose that's because I'm a farmer myself and understand better than most what it means to it lot of these poor beggars. And then I had my eyes opened by . those Boota people. I shall never forget how good Mrs. Boota was to me that day when I was carted to tho farm, and afterwards, when I'd come on by the train, after throwing myself down on the veld and believing it was all over with me. Mrs. Boota may be Doer by marriage and upbringing, but I fancy she's English still at heart. Her son was ' is t the same, and I should think the old man is p , too. If they're like that I shouldn't be surprised to learn that there are others like 'cm, too. As I say, , I'm not clever at reckoning things up, and can °align by what I've seen. I've seen both bad and good ; and I think it's only fair to forget the 'tad as much as I can, and think of the good. That's the sort of thing time parson tells us to do, isn'_t it, even if he doesn't always do it himself? Do you happen to know what the war's about? answered Lizzie, frankly. "But then I'v• had no time to think. It 's been all I could do to mind my own business. • "That's queer," said Mark. "I never came lerois anybody that did know. However, I suppose it's all right. But do you know, Liz, I've made up my mind on one thing—if I get out I;ellarnaevmeredatalkief thawypegninvye , me one." , of of rt Itiiimse countryw.ork i all , I've "That. won't bother mile," said Lizzie, quietly. : e doneriright, or t, "There are so many things I've seen that I never want to be reminded of, and if you put your medal away --acre I should never see it I shouldn't be tarry." "I've had a lot of time for thinking lately," con- ' tinned Mark, with deep conviction, "and l've made my mind upon this—they'd no business to let the Yeomanry come out here. What we're fur..

===== 0002601_19011220_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
1 0l

as I always understood it, is to look after the old country and see that no foreigner comes cadging after it, to drive us into the sea, and generally do for us. No able-bodied man could refuse to serve on such conditions. But instead of making us really fit to do that the War. Office rushed in and persuaded a lot of us to come out here. Now, I'm not sharp, but it seems to me that the people here tried to do just what-I should have helped to do myself—drive the lot of us into the sea. It's a pity, for their own sakes, that they didn't confine their operations to fair fighting." - "Well, it's no use worrying," persisted Lizzie, unable with success to reason with the patient. "You're a soldier, you k.now, and all you have to do is to obey. You' e no responsibility in the matter." .._ • .

"I'm gladl of that,' said Mark. "I shouldn't like to have. All I want now is to be able to wash my hand's of the whole business. When I think of decent people like the Bootas I almost feel glad that I'm pretty well out of it." "But they're not all that sort,r said Lizzie. "I suppose if they had been there wouldn't have been any war. But • I daren't stop any longer. I've talked too long as it is." "All right," said Mark, cheerfully. "Don't let me keep you. You're not the missis yet, and so I can't rush you about"and have all your attention. What am I to do if those bone-sawing chaps come round unawares? I don't half like that Donald.. He looks as if he'd got designs on me." "Do just' nothing. Leave everything to me," answered Lizzie, amazingly encouraged by Mark's good spirits. • "That'll suit me fine, for I'm feeling lazy," said Mark. "You get on with your business, Liz. Don't bother about me—l'm all right. Here' S your friend, the Scotty, comin' up." "Well," said the surgeon, as he reached Mark's side, "how do you feel now ?" "A lot better, sir," said Mark. "Humph!" muttered Donald, rather disgustedly, for the patient's.looks confirmed his words, and the surgeon had made up his mind that the only hope of real and lasting cure lay in the removal of the damaged limb. "I think I told you that I come of a pretty tough lot, didn't I, sir?" continued Mark, amiably. "I don't remember 'it," replied Dunald, rather sourlyl; "but I can quite liAlieve it." "We keep up a good dying average," said Mark; "it's been about eighty for both men and women. My mother's grandmother lived-to be ninety-nine. She was very proud of it, and would have gone off a lot • easier if she could have reached the three figures. She did her best, but just missed." The surgeon rubbed his chin. There was some- thing very interesting about this patient ; certainly it was strange and wonderful that he should make so light of a very serious,injury. He very carefully examined the Wound again. 'And you say you actually had this to bear with all the time you were escaping?" "Yes. sir," Mark assured him. • "And that it doesn't trouble you now?" "Well, not much to speak of." answered Mark. "But I don't understand," said the puzzled surgeon, whose theories and experiences we out- raged. "Theoreticall you know, you ought to be in a, bad way. Most men would have died under it."

"I suppose it's a matter of bringing up," said Mark. 'There are some people who think it pretty bad to get up at eight o'clock in the morning; but it never struck me as being hard that I should have to be out in the fields by four." "Yes, but haymaking isn't surgery," said Donald. , _ _

"Put it another way, then: think how"a lot of of people squirm about getting a tooth jerked out— s matter of a second; but I've heard my father say that folk used to think nothing of going to the blacksmith's and getting dragged round the forge with the pincer in their jaws before the tooth was out."

"What you think is," said Donald, brusquely, "that what would maim or kill another man won't bother you?" "That's exactly it, air," agreed Mark. I "Humph ! " again muttered the surgeon. "That's one way of looking at it. It has the novelty of freshness."

"And the rightlway, sir, I hope, "said Mark, eagerly. "We shall see," answered Donald, walking slowly off. "1 do believe the young fellow's right, he added to himself, again with his hand to his chin. "p it really did come out as he says, certainly I ought tp publish some notes on the case, as an extra/ordinary example of the curative povrec,of pluck and constitution." A few monrts later Lizzie rejoined Mark. "It's only for a econd," she said. "I can't stay. What does he say now? I saw him come." "Won't say anything •, left most of the talking to me," answered Mark. "Look here, Liz," he added, eagerly. "I distrust him more than ever. I'm certain he thinks he ought to have it." Mark nodded significantly towards the injured limb. "Not if I can help it," said Lizzie, still in a whisper, but a very hard and unbending one. "Now I'm off again; don't you bother." "As if I should," murmured the patient, "when 1 know that she's about. He may be sharp, but he'll lrlve to be very sharp, indeed, .to get ahead of Liz."

CHAPTER XXII.

GRIPPING A CHANCE. It became now more than ever a case of Lizzie guarding her patient, as if some ravenous ghoul were watching for a chance to swoop upon and devour him.

Mark's quaint conceit that Donald was at large, coveting the damaged limb, burnt into her memory, and She never lost sight,of it. That thing should not be done so long as she had the slightest power to prevent it; and as she had been successful so far, she hoped and 'prayed to be successful still. But she soon discovered cause for alarm.

Donald, excellent person though he was in many ways, was yet astonishingly susceptible in others. Nothing concerned him more than his professional pride; this was his all, his god, and he could not endure 'to have it so much as touched by an out- sider—and that outsider a mere layman, too. In some subtle way he felt that Mark,had set himself up against him, and for the present had scored. The more he thotglit'of this the more he was satisfied of its truth, and in proportion to his conviction was the sting he felt. Donald's annoy- ance turned 'to exasperation at having been outwitted--"by a simple countryman, too," he growled within himself—and ended in a- piqued resolve to get the business done. "It shall come off to-morrow," he said. "We can't manage it to-day, with so much else to do. Lizzie, I daresaY, will cut up rough about it; but she'll live to be grateful, to me. It isn't, either, as if my view weren't shared by the other men." 1 This was a lathe attempt to satisfy himself that there was nothing shabby or ungenerous in what he had in his mind, and that it was solely a matter of compelling everything to give place to surgical expediency. "After all, too, I don't see why a farmer in a comfortable way shouldn't be able to get through life on one leg as well as on two." This w'ris completely convincing, and Donald resumed his duties with a restful mind. Lizzie, quick-witted at all times, and more than quick-witted now—the situation had made her positively crafty—comprehended from Donald's demeanour that something was seriously amiss ; she understood fully, from a remark he made, that the opinion of the profession must take his course, and hinted as to the time. Satisfied now beyond doubt, Lizzie set to work to put .into force a plan vhich had come into her active znin44that morning. That day there was to leave the hospital a batch of men who were well enough to travel to the coast and be invalided home. 'So full was the building, and so pre.ssed for space were the staff; since new cases were constantly arriving, that excessive energy was being shewn tO get the convalescents away. It was one of the surgeon's common sayings—he had a good many sayings which had survived tho usage of years, but which answered his purpose well enough—that a man could not be in two places at once, and could not do a couple of things together. ' Luckily for Livie's scheme, these ancient saws were particularly true ; the surgeon could not be attending his own bard duties and keeping a watchful eye on either Mark or Lizzie, nor could he see that certain arrangements, were net being carried out precisely according to instructions. Amid so much hurry and pressure, it was inevitable that there should be confusion, and that one or two things should go wrong. Here a nurse, overwrought and overworked, might get astray in fulfilling her duties, there an orderly, buffeted and bewildered, might do, the wrong thing at a critical moment, and add to the disarrange- ment and confusion. These things happened in spite of, every care; ' and it was because they happened that 'Lizzie got her inspiration. I Looking front the hospital, Lizzie saw Donaldi walking towards his canvas dwelling in the medicali (Akers tents. She watched him turn as if he

meant to go to the isolation camp, and turn with and make his way in the direction of the encamp- ment of the hospital orderlies. Now or never was her time. An ambulance train, was on the neighbouring railway, already nearly full ; the last of the patients were being hurriedly got out of the hospital and put on board. Lizzie hastened to Mark's bed. "Don't ask a single question," she whispered. "Do just as you're told. You're going away—not a word, for God's sake! It's all Wit." • Mark repressed an exclamation of amazement. But for Lizzie's assurance he would have judged that so far front being all right, it was very seriously wrong. But he was well schooled by this time, and held his peace. A surprise mora or less in thesa stirring days was neither here nor there. Just then there was no one about. The beds hearest to him had been emptied, and the doctors and nurses were in other quarters. Suddenly there appeared a worried non-commissioned officer with a couple of orderlies bearing a stretcher. "It's dashed queer!" exclaimed the sergeant, saving himself, on seeing Lizzie, from using a harder adj eel ife. The nurse's mind was already made up. "Come, be quick, -don't idle there all day,' she said. "Take this man to the train." . _

The sergeant gave an exclamation of joy. "Why, hero's the very one !" 'he said. "There's one inissin', an we've been half round the hospital lookin for him. Hook him lip, boys—there, steady, now. You look pretty well, matey; wish I could change places with you. Don't I just wish I was goin' for a jaunt on the salt water, instead of stickin' here ? But, never mind, my turn'll come. I shall be as badly knocked up as a dead mule if this sort of thing goes on. Got 'ha all right, boys ? That's the ticket ! Thanks, sister, for givin' me the tip. Could'ilt tell where the missin' link had got to, an' was bep,innin' to think he must ha' titkon up his bed an' walked home. Now then, no grinnir '; you chaps--an' don't shake the bones out of the man "

"Good-bye," said Murk, rising a little. He almost said "Liz," but instantly remembering that lie must neither ask questions nor slim surprise, he checked himself. Before he could collect his startled senses, he had been put carefully into the :rain and was already leaving the neighbourhood of the hospital. The sergeant, vastly relieved, expressed his feelings to the orderlies. "If I'd had a couple of cabbage-headed guinea-pigs under me," he said, "I might have expected 'em to walk past the very bed where the chap was that we wanted; but with two able-bodied men, I fancied the case would be different. It was the softest thing I ever saw to see you two starin' about you with no more wit than a' infant. I've no hope for you, Dukes, nor yet you, Slater." "nut," protested Dukes, "I didn't know,it was the chap. Yond's a Yeoman—as I understood, we wanted a gunner." "I tliought it was a 'One Guards chap we was wantin'," said Slater.

, "Yes," sneered the sergeant, "there you go; gunner an' 'Orse Guard. What licks me is that you weren't lookin' about for a prince or a Field- Marshil I"

_ "Anyway,' said Dukes, "I'm certain o' one thing, an' that isn't two—whatever we was lookin' for, it wasn't one o' them Yeomen chaps." "I'm certain o' that, too," said Slater, cor- roboratively. The sergeant for a moment looked troubled and puzzled. But he brightened up instantly. "Well, what I'd got to do was to make the numbers tally, an' I did that. Besides, if that Yeoman wasn't goin' down to the coast, he ought to ha' been. He wasn't a case for hospital any longer, an' if you'd aeon as much as I've seen, you'd know. Now then, sharp; fall in, an' let's get back." But sin the return the sergeantgain felt troubled, and his misgivings grew so eatly that on re- entering the building he sough izzie and said: "There seems to be 3 bit o' doubt about that Yeoman' chap. I hope it's all right. I hope he'd got to go." "Oh, yes; he'd got to go," replied Lizzie, emphatically; and the sergeant instantly dismissed all his doubts and fears. _ _ _ .

"Of course, I knew it," he said, hastily. "Only ,those jackasses had got the idea that they were lookin' for somebody else." "How strange I" said Lizzie, smiling. "It's surprisin' how strange some o' their ideas are," said the sergeant, who was of a somewhat gallant disposition, and rather judged,tnrom the nurse'.s looks, that lie was making a favourable impression upon her. "If you'd seen as much of 'em as I've seen, you'd agreerwith me,. I daresay;" "I'm sure you're right, sergeant,"tsaid Lizzie, again smiling. The sergeant was both flattered and encouraged. He smiled also, and shewed such a marked dis- position to continue the talk that Lizzie found it necessary to get rid of him. "There now," she said, pleasantly, "you've done so well that there's nothing left here for you to do just Inv ; but I fancy they're wanting TOll over there." The sergeant was an acute man. He took his dismissal bravely, having satisfied himself that the nurse was not quite as charming as she might have ,been, and was stuck up beyond the just rights of her position. But before he went he said: "If there should' have been any mistake—not that I think there has, of course—l hope you'll make it clear that I'm not to blame. I had to square my heels before the doctor only yesterday, and I'm not in a hurry to do it again." • "Certainly," said Lizzie, readily. "If there was any mistake, it was mine.; I shall make that clear." "Dr. Donald's the one to be reckoned with," said the sergeant as lie left. "I'll remember that," answered Lizzie. As soon as she was alone shelsat on the edge of -the bed, and felt hot and cold by turns when she thought of the position in which she had placed both herself and Mark. "It's no good trying to say that it was a mistake," she said. "He wouldn't believe it. And it's no good trying to prevent it from being found out—and very soon. Whatever shall I do? " She clasped her bands and rose to her feet. "Whatever shall I do?" she repeated. She tapped the flooi angrily and thought wildly of all sorts of subterfuges—of getting another patient into the bed insti lily, of arranging the pillows and bedclothee ap that they looked like a man; but she knew how -hopeless it was to attempt to practise any such deception. . :Pie was in the extremity of her bewilderment when she heard quick footsteps.. Almost as soon she heard the startled voice of Donald. ' "Where's he gone?" he demanded. "What's the meaning of this? Come, nurse, this looks like a very serious business." "He's gone off with the train," said Lizzie, with a white face.

"With the train—down to the coast ?" said the doctor, speaking rapidly. "And—and—you've done it ?"

"Yes, I liace," said Lizzie. "What else could I do ? "

..„ . "But—but it's absolutely unheard of," exclaimed 35onald. ."You've put yourself in a very grave position. I'm afraid there's only one way of dealing with a case like this—and that would be v,.ry hard on you—after the work you've done. I'm really very, very sorry." s . ("If he is all right, I shan't cars about myself," said Lizzie, stubbornly. , Donald looked from her to the empty bed, and from the bed to her again. Despite his anger and amazement, he found that he was almost admiring the pluck and ingenuity which could wrench a patient in this way out of the very jaws of the operating theatre. Besides this, he had genuine admiration for Lizzie, whom he looked upon as the most wilful nurse in the hospital. , "I'm ....Altered if I know what to make of it," he continued. "You really have put both yourselr bald me in a most awkward position; and what of the patient ? He'll be dead before to-morrow." "I know better than that," said Lizzie ; "he won't die yet. Tell me one thing "—she went (spite close to Donald as she spoke—"are you going to have ljiin. fetched back ?" "Cert inly not," he exclaimed, with great energy, "The place will be glutted again in less than twenty-four hours. No, no, miss; you've made his bed, and he must li,e on it. That isn't what's bothering me—l'M concerned about you, that's all. I want to save you from yourself, if I can. I don't want you to be disgraced and sent about. your business." Donald knitted his brows, and seemed to be reflecting, hard. Once or twice ho cast a !Aleful glance at the empty bed and at. the nurse. Theri he smiled. He could not help it; the occurrence Yid so many features that were grotesque "Yes, young lady," lie said, "you've mane it this time." . , "I know," said Lizzie, encouraged by his manner. "I know I've done wrong; but I'm not sorry for it. What do you mean to do ?"' "I'm thinking," answered Donald, almost as quietly. He seemed to be thinking very hard,4 for he made no further utterance for several minutes. Then ke said: "I did think at first of undoing alit , ._

Vat mid been done, by way of shelving rtuthority must be paramount, and that the pros fession can't be set at nought in this way by a. young woman. Then I thought I'd be generous -- Donald was not magnanimous enough to confess, that he had concluded that the revelation of what had been done would make him and some of his colleagues look both foolish and neglectful—"and so I've determined to say'no more about it. You're a wilful woman, and, you know, a wilful WOlll3ll must have her way." "I'm sure it'll turn out to be all right." "I hope so," said Donald, grimly. "Anyway, the position is of your own making. You mustn't blame me, you know, if you find when you get married that your husband's been made a cantankerous creature for all his life through what you've done. I cast all the blame from me." "Never mind," answered Lizzie, brightly, "I'll accept it. It's mine, and I can bear it." , She went off joyously. As he watched her leave Donald muttered: "It's good thing all women are not like her. If they were, there would be no patients left for us tr Ftandle." . .

WATCHING THE STRUGGLE.

Sympathy from. Lancashire Workers.

Lancashire mill operatives are deeply interested in the legal struggle of the Welsh coal miners with the owners over the absentee issue. There is a areat deal oi sym- pathy with the miners, to 3, for in Lancashire the mill hands are also ready, at every favourable opportunity to escape from " active service" in the mills and work- shops to the exhilarating seaside resorts in order to recuperate consumed energy. The congenial cli nate ot Sunny Southport appeals largely to the workers of the County Palatinate, and thousands are invigorated by visiting this favourite watering-place. A nomewha; extracrdinary instance has just come to light where even the ozone-laden bretzes of Southport faded to do just what was required. Mr. James Quinn, of 11, Hollins-street. Stalybridge feundryman, has had this experience. Ile readily antft Intelligently related it to an " Oldbam Standard " re. Dotter, who waited upon him a few days ago. Mr. Quinn who is forty-seven years of age, is a native of Stalybridge, and, until recently, had spent the greater part of his working days in the mill and the fonndry. " For the pant twenty years," he said, " I have been plagued with indigestion, The principal cause of it was that I ran myself down when working in tho foundry. I used to sweat in front of the furnace, go home to dinner, and hurry back again, the varying temperature leaving its mark in the long run. I tegan to suffer from general debility and excessive weakaess, and got so much worse that I determined to see a doctor, who told me that if I did not at once go to Southpoit consumption would probably develop'. " Did you go to the seaside?" inquired the reporter. "1 went to Southport, staying three weeks in th,+ Convalescent Home there, but nothing did me so much good as Dr Williams' pink pills.lilcertainly felt a little better while at Southport, but it did not revive me like tbe pills." Mr Quinn went on to state that he felt as well to-day as ever as he had done, and, as ha certainly appeared to be in good health and Spitits.the reporter was tempted to probe into the nature of Mr Quinn's suffering. "Every time I ate anything," he explained, " I had wind on the stomach, and I had such a painful weight in that part that at first I used to force my food back again: • My strength gradually failed me, and I became very tired and depressed. Then I became so touch run down that the 'doctor feared that I would go into con- aurn ption.” " How did you first come to hear of Dr Williams' pink pills ?" the interviewer asked. " I happened to be complaining about my indigestion to a young fellow at Ashton, when he asked me, You think Dr Williams' pick pills will do you any gond ?' I started laughing at him, and thee answered, ' They are the Fame as everything else; ttey give temporary relief, and then you get as had as Ever. They are the same as all the pills—you take them and than you are jiggered again.' He told me he thought they were as good a remedy as I could get, and, thinking about what he had said, I ultimately determined to give them a trial. I began to take two pills three times a day, and when I had been taking them a week I felt great relief. I kept on with them, and they took the pain away. Of;course, I had to regulate my food at the same time, but I found that Dr Williams' pink pills did me a deal of good. I continued taking them until I had finished two boxes, when I felt in very fair form, and stopped taking them. I have no: needed to have any for the last three months,teing now free from indigestion, and deriving good benefit from my food. Miss Lydiate, who was present at the interview, and who reaidEs at the same addresa as Mr Quinn, con- firmed the etatements repro—duced above,and assured the reporter that the cure effected upon Mr Quinn by Dr Williams' pink pills bad been most remarkable. " Have you mentioned your cure to anyone? " thes reporter further interrogated. "Yes," was the reply. "They have cured Miss Lyrliate, who has suffered from dyspepsia, and I have pointed out to Mr Meld, a chemist of Oldham, how Dr Williams' pink pills have benefited me. He expressed the opinion that they were as good a remedy for indi- gestion as anyone could wish to have. I have also told a friend at Stalybridge about them, and he remarked that They certainly were well recommended.' I lost. no opportunity of telling my friends of what they have done for ice,' and Mr Quinn, in conclusion, gave the reporter full permission to publish or make what use ho p'eased of the particulars which he gave concerning the benefit derived from taking Dr. Williams' pink pills.

1-irnilton House School—Prize Distribution. There was an iateresting gathering in the Masonic Hall, Warrington, on Tuesday evening when the annual distribution of prizes to the children attending Hamilton House School, Bold- street, took place in the presence of a large attend- ance of parents and friende. At the outset of the proceedings Miss M Williams made a favourable report with reference to the work of the past year. 'The prizes were then distributed by the Rev John Yonge to the following pupils : IF:isle Taylor,-nausic prize and Oxford certificates ; Bessie Forshaw, sixth class prize, attendance prize, French prize and Oxford certificate; Marion 'Weaver, fifth class class prize, examination prize- and Oxford certificate; Connie Hall, fourth class prize; Dorothy Hall, third class prize and exam- ination prize; Russell Crampton, second class prize; Dorothy Bracecamp, conduct prize; Frank Bracecamp, attendance prize. Having presented the awards Mr Yonge delivered a short speech, daring the course of which haexpressed regret that the donor of the music , prize (Rev W BracecamD> was with them for the last time at such a gathering He regretted very much Mr. Bracecamp's re- moval from the town and he was sure the same regret was shared by many people in Warrington. They would follow him with interest and sym- pathy in the new sphere to which he had been called. The speaker, continuing, offered to present the music prize in future, lie also offered earnest, and sincere congratulations to the principals of the school on the successful position it was already attaining in the town. A. school of that kind was very much needed, and he was very glad to find is had met with such substantial success. (Applause.) —Ths prize distribution having been concluded, an operetta entitled "Prince Ferdinand" F was pre- sented by a number of the pupils. The characters were well sustained by the following : Prince Ferdinand. ;NOTES ON UAMES AND ATHLETICS RUGBY FOOTBALL

By " Ovalist." Warrington surprised football enthusiasts in two ,counties, and upset all the calculations of the news- paper prophets by defeating Oldham at Watershed- dings on Saturday. A few faithful admirers of the Warrringtou team took advantage of the excursion to Spindiedom and on arrival found the hilly town suffer- ing from a recent snowstorm which had bereft every telegraph and telephone pole of its wires and made the streets a foot deep in snow. It was under these unfavourable conditions that ascent was made of the steep inclines which lead to the Watersheddings enclo• sure. Towering aboye were the spurs of the Pennine chain looking picturesque in their snow-capped maiffle of spotless white. The ground itself also bore evidetice of the recent storm. On one side the snow had been swept into heaps, and for a touch line a trench had been dug in the snow. To make matters worse, a biting wind swept across the ground, and was a 4 much advan- tage to the teana that had it behind them as it was detrimental to the team called upon to face it.

Considering the unpropitious elements there was a I very satisfactory attendance, over 5,000 being present.! Amongst the "rou,gliyeds '' there seemed to be a de- cided confidence in the ability of the "cherry and whites " to wipe; out the blotch on the Oldham escut- cheon which was sustained in October last at Wilders- pool. One enthusiast was demonstrating his confid- ence by offering long odds that Oldham would win with a margin of six points. There were certainly grounds for this confidence. In the memory of the oldest spectator there was not one occasion when War- rington had retired victorious from the Watersheddings enclosure. The wirepullers had either been defeated or staved off defeat by a draw. Since 1895 these com- binations have played 14 matches, and Oldham have won all of them with the exception of three, which were lost at Warrington. On Saturday, a brand new three-quarter in the person of Arthur Hinge was placed in the Oldham team and there were great expe4ations respecting him. He is a tall wiry youth, with plenty of dash, but on Saturday i cannot be said he covered himself with glory. He may do better' as he becomes more accustomed to the. Oldham players.

Isherwood acted wisely, when on winning the 'toss he chose to have the stiff breeze behind his men in the first half. By its aid he was able to press his opponents almost the whole of the 35 minutes and to obtain a lead of five points at the interval. With the exercise of a little more precision in the giving and accepting of the ball from one player to another, the score might have been largely augmented. But the half-gale militated against accurate play and although Fish missed one or two openings he can hardly be blamed. Sometimes the wind blew the ball over his head and at others it went rolling along ,the ground instead of find- ing a place under his arm. To his credit, Fish scored the whole of the five points for Warrington and this so chagrined an Oldham lad tha he was heard to re- mark that "the Warrington team is all Fish." This is hardly true hut other teams besides Oldham have felt the sting of the finny creature., '

When Oldham had the wind behind them fti. the second half it was generally felt that the Warrington citadel would be lowered. The Oldham forwards went away with great dash and if the back division had shown greater resourcefulness there is ro doubt the result might have been different, but they didn't. On the afternoon's play, the Warrington backs completely outclassed the Oldham back division. In the forwards Oldham had the advantage, but the backs were mediocre and they were easily prevented from scoring. One could not help'contrastingithe back play of Oldham with that of previous years when such men as Hurst and McCutcheon,Davies and Fletcher,struck terror into the hearts of their opponents. But times have changed and one was inclined to remark "How have the mighty fallen," after witnessing the display of the -01Tham back division on Saturday.

The whole of the Warrington backs did themselves justice under great disadvantages and it is difficult to single out individuals. Hallam was quite as resource- ful as his vis-a-vis and both played uncommonly well. On one occasion it looked as if Hallam would score himself but he unselfishly threw across to Fish but that player was held up on the line. Fish has hardly shown the same dexterity in getting possession of the ball since his injury to his hand, which still troubles him: Harris was twice pulled up near the line and his turn of speed stopped Spottiswood from scoring on one °ed.- .sion. Both centres played a sound game and if one were inclined to find fault perhaps Dickenson might be charged with holding the ball a trifle too long on one or two occasions. Townson's play in the half-back division was closely watched and a favourable opinion was formed of him. He is full of resource behind the pack awl when he has an opening he feeds the backs unselfishly. His weakest point is in tackling but on - Saturday he had plenty of practice in this department and it was rarely that an opponent got the better of him. He is not a man to be spoken of in the same breath as " Smack " Bate, but he is certainly a player of promise and will justify the committee's selection ere long. Allen played one of his characteristic gaines,but there were some misgivings respecting him when he was penalised so often in dangerous proximity to the War- rington goal. Our forwards were not quite equal to •the Oldham eight, which was somewhat surprising. Taylor's play on Saturday justified his inclusion.

It is really remarkable when one comes to think of it, that Warrington should take four points out of Oldham, and more remarkable still that they should be inferior forward and superior behind. What must it have cost the Oldham scribe .to pen the following con fession in the " Athletic News?" •"fhe Warrington backs proved more tactful and fleet of foot than those of their opponents,and their rerurcefulness, with prob- ably the heavy character of the ground, were the lead- ing causes which conduced to victory." Such a com- ment should prove to our committee that the backs would do very well were the forwards stronger. Never since the Hunslet match on October 26th have the Warrington forwards lifted up their heads, so ro speak. I unhesitatingly say that in every match since : Brig- house Rangers, St. Helens (home and away) Halifax, Batley and Oldham they have had the worst of the forward argument. And yet some members of the committee say "it is not forwards we want but a class half-back." They want ;forwards more than any- thing.

Rather curiously the League leaders failed to get the better of the wooden-spoonists en Saturday at 'Brig- house, the match ending in a draw. Are the Rangers cracking up? We shall see when they come to War- rington. Hull are eood seconds and on Saturday defeated Halifax easily. Swinton made a "holy show" of Batley and crossed the cup-holders' line no fewer than six times—and Warrington cotild not get across once at Batley. Leigh had hard luck at Bradford and Huddersfield gave Hunslet a close game.

The League table now stands as follows : For

L ALL 1-LIGILTS itESEIWED.

MISS TUTTLE'S MISTAKE,

- BY G. B. BURGIN. sfrk

Miss Tuttle was undeniably pretty: that was the worst of it; and she did things on the strength of her prettisese and 3ilusapness, Which would not have been tolerated .tor asi instant if they had been per- petrated by a snub-tweed. girl with a resemblance,ta Pharaoh's lean kine. •‘ Very well," was Miss Tuttle's-reply to her Mamma, when remonstrated -with on the subject -of her cruelty to young inei. "What are .yonag men for ? If they don't like it, they-can lump- it.' The expression was not ele- gant, butits general effect was to cause Miss Tuttle's admirers to "lump " each other with their fists, to the keen delight of that young lady, who was as unfeeling as ehe was pretty. " You ought to be 'ashamed of yourself," said. Miss Tuttle's Mamma, to her, about a Week before. Christmas. "Even this season of peace and good- will doesn't seem to have any effect on you. When I was .e. girl—and a very pretty girl your papa thought me—l didn't dare to go on so." "You see, Mammy," said Miss Tuttle, with an impertinent toss of her pretty head, " that was some time ago." "-Fortunately for you, Miss," retorted the indig- nant Mrs. Tuttle, "or you would have a good whipping." " Fancy your talking like that at this season of peace and goodwill!' Why, I'm too big to whip now, you cruel old- parent," said Miss Tuttle' se- renely. "I wonder you dare to recall your PA wickedness in that direction."

"It would be just as well if.you were to recall it ahe oftener," said Mrs. Tuttle. ".As I said just now, here Christmas coming, and yo; set all the young men by the ears instead of' promoting peace -and good-willamong them."' "I'm sure I do all I can to make them happy," said Miss Tuttle. "It isn't my fault if they will go quarreling with one another, and making love to me, and as to Christmas being a time of peace and good-will, I should like to make them all happy but I don't see how it's to be done. When I try to make one of them happy, all the others call him • names." It's your way of mismanagin,,,, things," said Mrs. Tuttle, severely. "When I was a young woman' I never allowed more than two people to make love to me at once. The one who didn't mean anything by his attentions, always kept the other up to the mark." "Well, you see, it's so much simpler to have half -a-dozen," said Miss Tuttle, serenely. " You can't always depend on one man being in a good temper. If you have five or six others to fall back upon, it makes things so much pleasanter all round. Everybody gets a chance then, and feels as if he had something to live for." "It may sound all very well in theory," said Mrs. Tattle, " but it won't work in real life. Now, you. are already beginning to get on in years and ought to settle down. If you don't aecept one of these ex- ceedingly eligible young men who are eating us out of house and home—(last Sunday's meat bill was simply preposterous)—you may find yourself an old maid, and you won't relish the prospect." "I don't know. There is always a goo& deal df I fun poked at old maids; but they get very much the best of the bargain if they've enough money to live on." " Yes but that is just what they haven't," said Mrs. Tuttle. ." Nowadays, everybody has to work for a living." " And that, I suppose, is why people marry.. Girls have to work for their husbands in order to keep them in a good temper and all that sort of thing. Well, I'm not going to marry anybody just yet, and if these reople come bothering me, I shall just tell them what I think of them." ' " Pity you can't select any one of them," said Mrs. Tuttle. " Here's Christmas coming on, and I can't bear to see you upsetting all the young men in the place and making them have uncharitable thoughts of one another. They'll get to look upon Christmas as a hollow mockery.'' "Christmas doesn't seem to. cause any difference to them," said Miss Tuttle. "They quarrel pretty much on an average all the year round, and the in- digestion caused by the things they eat here seems to make them more bitter than usual with one an- other."

"Well, your father and I have agreed we won't haveany more of-this nonsense. You must just pick one out of the half-dozen and stick- to him for better or worse. We can't go on like this, feeding every- one in the place without having any adequate return for our money, so to speak. Your Papa-says that you are an investment upon which he never gets any interes,t." •‘ I guess I can look after my own interests, and as to your wanting to get rid of me, I shall stay here just as long a, I lik • and marry whom I please; You shouldn't say such things to me at Christmas, you dear, unfeeling old Mammy, you." "You are a very undutiful girl l" said Mrs. Tut- tle, going off in a huff. This conversation set Miss Tuttle, thinking. It was true that half-a-dozen young men had failen in love with her pretty face ;' and it was equally true that she had not made up her mincl/which one out of the six she ought to marry. On Christmas Eve, whilst she wasstill in this un- decided-frame of mind, Miss Tattles six admirer& came in to spend the evening with her just- as the bells were beginning to ring. Miss Tuttle noticed that their countenances were illumined with the light of a stern resolve, and could not help thinking, that she had never seen six handsomer young men. It seemed such a pity to make five of them unhappy that she could not help regretting that she must de- cide in sonic way. She noticed, however, that when Mr. and Mrs. Tuttle had left the room, they did not break out into their customary light and airy con- versation, but sat stiffly erect. All of them had their hair cut exceedingly short: the one with a beard, had shaved it off; and, with a sinking of the heart, Miss Tuttle noticed that their moustaches were pointed at the ends in the approved Kitchener fashion. What on earth did it mean ? She was not long left in doubt as to their mean- ing, for Harry Singleton, the handsomest of the six, gave a preparatory cough, and said that, on behalf of the other five, he wished to askher•aquestion. " I hope it isn't a riddle. You aro not going to. -propose to me again this evening ? " apprehensively. enquired Miss Tuttle. " I am so tired of refusing you all, that you might give me a rest-on Christmas Eve when we should have nothing but.- kindly thoughts of one another. Mother has been making you a mince pie each in which to forget your mutual animosities. Now, don't take away yons,appeue„ by proposing to me, but be good boys, and. sing carols and eat nuts and tell me creepy ghost stories,. just as you did last year. But you all look so stern.: What is the matter? Don't, please don't, propose." " No, we are not going to propose to you, that is, not in the usual way," Said Harry Singleton; " but we have a proposal—a sort of collective pro- posal, as it were—to make to you. Perhaps you are not aware that we are all going to the Pliont 2" "Going where? You usually do go to the front when you come here. lam sure Mamma would never dream of asking you to. come in at the back door, although it would be so much more convenient sometimes when Papa doesn't want to see you." Harry looked at her sternly. "We have kradu- ally come to the conclusion that you have very little heart at all and that, as six good men and true, we should not be justified in wasting our lives on a flirt." _ _ _ - _ .

'I am not a. flirt," said Miss Tuttle, indignant- ly " and if you're all going to be disagreeable you had better go home again without your mince pies, so there !' But she looked inclined to cry, and seemed to thiuk that something unpleasant was com- ing.

"The fact is," said Harry Singleton, pausing dra- matically, in order to give due effect to his an- nouncement, " have all volunteered for the Front. and we all ieEsapTses la'e'cbshetrhie,l"aanlell fel‘t‘faWinltia. t thare her little chickens " were about to desert her. She could scarcely realise the fact, then looked into their stern resolved faces, and felt that Ishe would never see them again. "What do you want to go to the F-front for F" she asked, in trembling tones. " You—you—you'd much better stay at the back. It—it's co much safer." • "We are going to the Front because you are al- ways putting us into the background," said Harry Singleton, ominously. "We have come to the con. einsion that we had much better serve our country than waste our time here whilst you are endeavour. trig to make up your mind which of us you will ren- der uncomfortable for life." • " Don't be rude," said Miss Tuttle. "On Christmas Eve, too, to say a nasty epigrammatic sort of thing like that. Because you have volun- teered, is no reason why you should insult me. If I have caused you to volunteer, I have done the country a service for which it might to be grateful." "I suppose you mean," said Harry," that Eng- land ought to be grateful to get rid of us ?" "I think so." said Miss Tuttle. _.l.l'eople_who

can oe rude to a lady are just as well our of the country, among their fellow bores. You can go and fight for all you are worth; I will have nothing to do with any of you." "Oh. yes, you, will," said Harry, with meaning. " We have arrived at the conclusion that as most of us are likely to be killed, the survivors will come back and you can make your choice from them. Still, as we are going to the field of battle, we do not intend to go without something to which to look forward."

" C—c—can't you look forward to me ?" faltered Miss Tuttle. " 1

" No " said Harry, "because in that case we should :111 be in the same position as before. We have therefore arranged that aU of us who are killed will come back to haunt you next Christmas. I dare- say," he added gloomily, "that most of us will be killed, and then our gory ghosts will appear to you and—and you'll be sorry for the unfeeling way you have treated us." " Oh, don't !" said Hiss Tuttle. "Please don't. -It would be so exceedingly uncomfortable, and you'd see our Christmas Eve slipper and not be able to eat any of it. It is difficult enough for you to agree now; but if half of you become ghosts and half survive, I fancy that the relations will be more strained than before.'

" It doesn't seem to bother you much," sarcasti- cally remarked one of the others. "You know what a martyr I sin to lumbago. You're simply sending me to my death."

"Be quiet !" said Singleton, peremptorily, "and don't be rude to Miss Tuttle. What I propose is, that before we start she shall agree to marry the sur- vivor of us. As for myself, I have a rooted convic- tion that there will be only the remnant of one of us left."

"I can't marry a remnant," said Mips Tuttle, with spirit. " Don't talk to me as if you were left over from a sale, but sit down and be nice and Christmassy and sing me songs of Araby or any other music you've brought with you. Why can't you all stop quietly at home and let me make up my mind when I have time ?"

"Because," said another of her admirers, "we are not likely to live more than a hundred years. At the exceedingly leisurely rate at which you are making up your mind, we should most of 'us die of oll•age before you came to any decision." "I don't know why you are all so abominably rude to-night," faltered Miss Tuttle. " Here's Christmas coming on, when everything should be pleasant and comfortable, and you are upsetting all its pleasant traditions by your unpleasant behaviour. Do have a mince pie each, and let bygones be by- gones."

"We mean that we start in a week," said Single- ton, disregarding her piteous appeal, "and that most of us expect to be killed. If we are, we shall come back and haunt you." "But I don't want to be haunted," said Miss Tuttle, with decision. "It's all very well to be haunted in Christmas stories, but in real lifd it would be most unpleasant; it would send such a creepy-crawly feeling down my back. Besides, I can't have your ghosts dgetting about my room and telling me how you died and all that sort•of thing. There are plenty of other 'girls in the place. Why can't five of you go and propose to five of them P"

" I never thought of that," said admirer Num- ber Two.

"Nor I," said Number Three. "Nor I," said Number Four. "Nor I," said Number Five.

"You see !" said Miss Tuttle. "When you come to think over the matter sensibly, there is really no difficulty. This is Christmas time—a time of universal peace and goodwill. Now, you five just go oil and make love to the nicest girls you can think of, and, let me settle the other patt of the mat- ter with Mr. Singleton." Five of the young mai looked exceedingly foolish. They had not thought ofsthis solution'' of the diffi- culty, and fancied that they were making them- selves ridiculous. On a wave of the hand from Singleton, in whom they had implicit faith, they .filed out of the room with a vain attempt to look military and reproachful at the same time. Then Singleton, who remained, looked at Miss Tuttle, and Miss Tuttle looked at Singleton. Singleton thought that she had never been more lovely ; Miss Tuttle thought Singleton singularly handsome. "I suppose," said Singleton, with tragic em- phasis, "you don't mind the anguish you may cause to five other girls by this inconsiderate suggestion of yours? These fellows seem to do everything you tell them, no matter whether they like it or not."

"Well," said Miss Tuttle, "why don't you go and d 6 the same? There are plenty of girls in the wlace. You have only to follow their example and pick out one and then it can't matter to me whether you go to South Africa or stop at home." "No, I 4uppose not," said Singleton, moodily switching his leg with the light whip he carried in his hand, after the manner of a recruiting sergeant who wishes to impress an admiring crowd. "Of course you don't really care if I'm slain upon the field of battle. I—l--I've not been very well late- ly," (lia• looked the beau ideal of vigorous man- hood) "and it wouldn't take much to settle me. Well, I might perhaps worry through. What would you think of me if I came back with one arm and sine leg gone, and a Victoria Cross ?" "I should not be in a position to think," said Miss Tuttle. " If you take my advice, you will pro- pose to some other girl, and she will do all the think- ing necessary in the circumstances." " And you call this a Christmas spirit F" asked Singleton- bitterly, seeing that Miss Tuttle was not to be moved by his pathetic picture. "You are the most unfeeling girl I have ever had anything to d. with."

Miss Tuttle turned her face to the fire. "I am sorry you think so badly of me. I have been trying very hard to make the six Of you happy; and I don't appear to have succeeded in any way." Singleton looked surprised. "I didn't even know that you had been trying." Then be suddenly clasped her in his arms, kissed her again and again. and went away to the Cape, with the Christmas bell, ringing in his ears, and leaving Miss Tuttle bathe?, in blushes. Presently the blushes gave way to smiles. " I had no idea that waxed moustaches were so ticklyt" she murmured to the row of neglected mince pies; "I do hope that horrid old Kruger won't hurt him." Then she fell into a reverie, and the chiming of the bells seemed to change to a wedding march.

• Although Singleton went away, Miss Tuttle, as the months rolled on, found, that Pve of her admirers still remained in England. She could not account for it until, meeting one of them two months before Christmas, she asked him why he had not gone to .‘ The Iront."

The youth in question, one Harvey Shillingford, looked exceedingly shamefaced, although previously to his meeting Miss Tuttle he had seemed radiantly happy. He cast his eyes on the ground and shufibltd his feet in an uneasy way. " I am afraid," be said, " that we owe you an apology for having deceived you. We all thought that you didn't care a button for any of us, and so agreed to enlist. Then five of us backed out at the last moment, after taking your advice about the other girls, you know, and Singleton was the only one who really went to the Cape. He said we had treated you shamefully, and were a set of miserable sneaks, but that you were the only girl in the world for him, and if he couldn't win you, he'd gladly die on the —on the battlefield. He's been badly wounded I hear."

"You are a paved of cowards I" said Miss Tuttle bitterly. " He is the only gentleman of the six, and 'I sent him to his death. I wonder you didn't go out Ito look after him, instead of neglecting your duty to ;your country and stopping at home just to amuse. ;yourselves with a party of foolish girls. I can never be oufficiently thankful that I would have nothing to do with any of you," and she swept away in a whirlwind of indignation. During the course of the next week her five ad- mirers were married, and Miss Tuttle sent them wed- ding presents, but did not call on tbetr wives. When she saw one of them in the distance, she turned aside, thinking of Harry Singleton, and how welt he had behaved, for he had written to her father to say that his wounded tam was well and that, although ho had not yet won the Victoria Cross, he had gained a commission in Brabant's Horse, -Which was the next best thing to it. When she rememtered the moustache incident (in- deed she seldom forgot 4t) Miss Tuttle was fantoo proud to write to lam ib reply; but, although she had read of heroines in fiction doing the same thing, she carefully studied the lists of the wounded, and breathed a little prayer of thankfulness every time she did not find his name. At last, however, she did find it. This time his wound was serious, for he was -Alit is technically known as a " lunger," i.e., he had received u bullet through his lungs, and nothing but tins most careful nursing could save him.

When Miss Tuttle realrsed this, she wept bitter tears, and regretted that she had not been brought up as a professional nurse. All she could do was to stop at home and weep when she thought of her own folly, and how Singleton had thrown away his life because he thought she did .not care for him. As she at by the fire on Christmas Eve, she could not help somewhat bitterly contrasting her last year's experiences with those of the present moment. Then, she had six admirers; now, the probability was that she _was without elgn one; and, looking intojiero.
