At last, she came . softly into the room with a plate of mince pies and put them on the table with a sidelong glance at Miss !Fettle, whose eyes filled with tears, although she pretended not to notice. Mrs. Tuttle looked somewhat wistfully at her pale- checked daughter. Miss Tuttle was prettier than ever with that refinement of prettiness which sorrow alone can give. "It does seem," said Mrs. Tattle, with a mourn- ful attempt at cheerfulness, " Us if things might be worse, and then again they might not. Last year we were all so merry and cheerful, although those young men did not eat their mince pies; now, five of them have homes of their own, and can ruin their digestions without our help."' Miss Tuttle flung her arms about her mother's neck and began to cry. Mrs. Tuttle, whose sternne.s was all on the surface, patted her gently on the back and comforted her. "There 1 There! There! Don't take on so. I'm sure it's all for the best." "And I'm sure it's all for the worst," said Miss Tuttle. "There was only one man out of the six who was worth anything, and now he's probably d —d—d—dead !" .

Mrs. Tuttle's attempts at consolation being time repulsed, she hastily went outside, wiping her eyes with the corner of her apron. Left to her own sad musings, Miss Tnttlersat in. the armchair which she had once occupied as a throne, and gazed into the fire. There was no light in the room. Outside the music of the bells mourn- fully told her of the mistake she had made in trifling with the feelings of the one true-hearted lover who had gone to the wars for her sake and for that of his country, while the other five had stopped at home in ignoble peace. Miss Tuttle could not help wiping away a tear as she gazed into the fire. When a cin- der fell out in the form ot a coffin, she felt convinced more than ever that she would never be happy again. The falling snow glimmered greyly against the windows as if it were vainly beating for an entrance into the warm room. Miss Tuttle got up in order to draw the curtain. Then it seemed to her as if she were going toward a world of ghosts. She hastily resumed her seat, her head resting upon her pretty hands, as her thoughts went back to the five young men who were most likely having all sorts of pleas- ant meetings on this particular evening. She could hear in imagination all the nice things those girls would say of her. Perhaps, now that the other girls were married, they would pity her loneliness. She couldalso hear her father's jovial voice in the ball as he put up branches of holly and mistletoe, and scratched his fingers with the prickly leaves. Even that did not disturb his good temper, for he made jokes to her mother about when they had stood under the mistletoe thirty years ago. As the bells rang sadly outside, it seemed to Miss Tuttle that she had for ever missed the chance of mingling in similar senti- mental scenes, and, taking up: the poker, she gave a ferocious lunge at a smouldering lump of coal. When the coal began to burn, Miss Tuttle dropped the poker with a clatter, and again returned to her dreary musings. Suddenly, that creepy-crawly feeling of which she had so much dread, began gradually to spread down her spine. She turned towards the door with a half-stifled shriek as if afraid of something. What was that coming noiselessly through the door towards her? A grey, palpable, shapeless form which, as it drew nearer, Raduallx assumed the semblance of a human being. Then Miss Tuttle knew that lightly-spoken words had borne fruit, and that this was her lover's ghost. At first, while the fear lasted, it seemed to Miss Tuttle as if her heart would stand still. Then, as she recognised Harry Singleton's pale face, and his large reproachful eyes sparkling with an unearthly light, a novel sensation took possession, of her. All her fear fled. She was no longer alone in the world. After abandoning all hope of seeing her lover again, it was a solemn comfort to meet him even as a ghost. She fell on her knees with a little entreating cry, and stretched out her hands towards him, as the ghost regarded her with sad imploring eyes. " So you have come back at last, Harry ?" she said, with tears in her voice. " I was sitting here, feeling so lonely and wretched, thinking how I had wasted my life. The bells outside seemed to mock me, and whisper how wicked I had been to throw away your true affection. For the rest of my life. I shall always be alone. But now that you have conic to me, say one word, tell me that you forgive me all my foolish mistake, and that, although you may never appear to me again, you will pardon the harm that I have done you." Then she burst into tears, and her head drooped lower and lower until it almost touched the ghost's feet. As the ghost made no sign, she raised her hands imploringly and entreated it not to go away. "If," she said, • you only knew how much I have longed to see you again or hear from you, you would never have kept silent so long. , I found out, when you kissed me, Harry, you were the only man I had ever loved. Now, lam left alone for the rest of my life to think of all I have lost through my folly. , Christmas makes me feel more bitterly what I have thrown away ; but you know it was not wickedness on my part, only thoughtlessness. I did love you, Harry, with all my heart, and I shall love your memory for ever." The ghost seemed surprised. Then, it fell on its knees beside Miss Tuttle and put a very real arm round her waist. " Don't you know me, dearest ?" Miss Tuttle gave a wild shriek and fainted. Mr. and Mrs. Tuttle burst into the room. " How did you get in here F" asked Mr. Tuttle severely. "I thought you were dead." " Well, last time I was here," said Harry, " your daughter told me it would be more convenient if I came round to the back instead of going in at the front. The back door was open, and you and Mrs:. Tuttle were so busy kissing each other under the mistletoe that I crept quietly into the parlour and found this dear girl here alone." Mrs. Tuttle rushed towards Harry Singleton and flung her arms round his neck. "Then you're not dead, after all, my dear boy ?" Miss Tuttle looked up through her tears. " I knew at once when his moustache began to t—tickle, that it couldn't be a ghost." She clutched Harry by the arm again to make sure that he really was alive, and. he kissed her before her father and mother in the most unblushing manner. " You're quite sure you're not dead ?" enquired Mr. Tuttle, in an unbelieving tone of voice. "You're thin enough to be dead !" "Of course, I'm not," answered Harry. " Can a dead man kiss people ?" And lie kissed Mrs. Tuttle no less heartily than she had kissed him. Then, as Mr. Tuttle seemedstill undecided, Harry's eyes fell on the mince pies. Can a ghost eat mince pies ?" he asked, in tones of real feeling. The next moment there was one mince pie less in the world. The disappearance of the mince pie convinced even Mr. Tuttle that Singleton was still alive. " How did you manage to get here just in time for our Christ- mas dinner ?" he asked. " You might have let us know about it."

"Well, you see,': said Harry, " I decided to give you a little surprise, and, as the last time I was here I did not feel particularly welcome, I thought per- haps, if I came in quietly and. apologised to your daughter for all the rude things I had said to her, she might forgive me. It was my mistake alto. gether.'' " No," said Miss Tuttle, with derision. " The mistake was wholly mine, . and if you will only for- give me, Harry, I will never forget it as long as I live."

The bells once more rang out, but, ob. how differ- ently. Their music was Charged with hopeand joy and love. As Harry took Miss Tuttle into his arms, the waits joined in with a merry chorus. whereupon Mr. Tuttle rushed into the garden to distribute the rest of the mince pies and entreated every red-nosed wait "to give it a name." The red-hosed ones gave it a name," and also, led by Mr. Tuttle, sup- plied three cheers for the future Mrs. Singleton. BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

Ax ACTIOR APPEARS AT THE POLICE COURT.- SINGULAR CONDUCT.

An actor named Stephen Holmes Robson, lodging at 27, Golborne-street, appeared at the Warrington Police Court on M mdav, on charges of wilfully breaking a number of panes of glass In the window of a house, 27. Golborne-street on Sunday night, and also with assaulting Rachel Turner,the tenant of the 11,use. He admitted breaking the glass and pleaded provocation, but pleaded not guilty to the assault.— hachael Turner said she kept a professional lodging-house at 27, Golborne-street. The prisoner had lodged there for four weeks. She had bad unpleasantness with him two or three times. On Sunday afternoon prisoner was going into the room of another lady, when witness forbade him to do so. He tried to enter the room forcibly, and she prevented him. He called her some very *ad names, and afterwards cast aspersions upon the characters of everybody in the house. He said he would knock witness's brains out, and be struck her on the ride of the head with his clenched fist. He was eventually persuaded togs into his own room. Witness was very frighteaed, and sent for a constable. An officer came and cautioned the prisoner. After that,she believed prisoner went to sleep, and at eight o'clock in the evening left the house. He returned about a quarter past ten. Witness heard a knock at the door, and on asking who was there received no answer. She opened the door and saw the prisoner. She told him he was trot to come into the house again. He pushed her backwards and forcibly entered the house. He closed the door. She opened it and called tol a lodger named Sergeant. With his help she got the prisoner into the street. She then closed the door and locked it. Prisoner swore and said he would enter the house by some means or other. He then smashed about eleven panes of glass in the window. Sergeant went out for a oonstable,but on his return prisoner had gone. Witness subsequently went to the Police Station and saw the prisoner. His right hand was covered with blood. He said he had broken the window in a lit of temper against his wife. Prisoner said he was very agitated when ,he went to the house in the afternoon. He was going to see a lady friend who was staying in the house, and he was prevented. A constable was afterwards brought in. He had been having a wordy argument with his wife, and Mrs Turner had interfered. He went out in the evening to look for other lodgings, and came back with the intention of taking away his belongings. He was entering the house when two men knocked him down and kicked him, he was sorry he broke the window. Mrs Turner struck him in the afternoon, and he retaliated by pushing her down two or three steps —He dist not strike her. —The magistrates Unposed a fine of 5s and costs (21 1.5 s 6d) or in default of payment 14 days' imprison- ment.

TRESPASSING ON THE CHESHIRE LINES RAILWAY STATION. —A WARNING T ) LOAFERS—AIfred Beckett, 37 Scotland- road and Patrick Richard Moran, 15 Pk.rtland-street, were summonedfor trespassing on the Cheshire Lines Railway at 'Warrington on the 26th and 28th November respectively. Defendants did not appear.—Superintendent Detective Simpson conducted the prosecution on behalf of the 0 L C. —William Bradshaw Taylor. a constable employed by the O L C, stated that on November 26th he saw the defendant Beckett on the platform of Central Station. Defendant said he had come there for newspapers. Witness requested him to leave the station but he refused to go, and witness had to eject him. Subsequently defendant returned and witness put him off again. Defendant made use of obscene language and threatened to take his (witness's) life with a knife. He had frequently had occasion to order defendant off the station. On the 28th November Moran was on the platform stopping passengers and asklng to be allowed to carry their luggage. He refused to leave the station when quested and witness put him o/, Defendant returned and witness ejected him a second time, Defendant was an habitual loafer and a buisance at the station.—Evidence was also given by William Cliffe, a cab driver.—The Chief Constable said defendants were weV-known to the police as station loafers and thieves. Beckett, who was known as "Lawyer Beckett." was 0"e of the beat-known loafers in Warrington and had repeatedly been sent away from the station. He was a perfect nuisance to passengers. Beckett had made six appearances and Moran eleven. Moran had been before the Court for theft and obstructing the police.—The Bench imposed a fine of 20s, and costs with the option of a month's Imprisonment• LICENSED VICTUALLER SUMMONED—Thomas Massey. licensee of the •• Waggon and Horses " Inn. DESTRUCTIVE FIRE AT WIDNES. Early on Sunday morning a serious fire broke out at the Albert-road Wesleyan School. It appears that about five o'clock on Sunday morning the wife of the caretaker, whose house adjoins the build- ing, noticed a strong smell of fire. She immedi- ately aroused her husband, and, on going into the cellar, he found the floor over the heating apparatus on fire. Ile gave the alarm to the fire station, and the brigade turned out under Deputy Superin- tendent Franklin,and on their arrivalit was found that the fire had secured a good hold and practi- cally the whole of the interior was in flames. After about an bout's playing on the fire, they succeeded in extinguishing the flames. The damage is esti- mated at about £5OO, which is covered by insur- ance.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. A New Organ at Buckley-street Chapel.

Special services were held in the Methodist Free Chapel, Buckley-street, in connection with the opening of the new organ. The service in the morning was con- ducted by Mrs Simpson, and in the evening ny Mr A. Corney. The choir, under the leadersnip of Mr J H Cook, and aasisted by an orchestral band, sang special authaia.arad two solos, " Abide wan Die " and "Lead, k ndly L'ght," were tastefully rendered by Madame F Christian. In the afternoon the ceremony of the open- ing of the new organ was performed. Mr A Jenniwtsr president of the church,after a few well-chosen remarks. presented Mis Pierpoint with the key of the organ. which was then opened on behalf of the church. The chairman (Coun W Pierpoirat) delivered an interesting address, alter which an organ recital was contributed in splendid etyle by Mr Percy Randall. Solos were given by Mrs Hannan, Miss Wilson,Madame F Christian, Mr F Warburton, and Mr J Hannan, and selections on the viola and violin by Messrs A Bates and B Butcher respec- tively. Miss Sutton and Mr Trow acted as accom- panists. There were large congregations, and the collections, in aid of tie organ fund, realised a sub- stantial sum.

LATUIFORD V. ORFORD BARRACKS. This Modal WAS played at the Barracks on Saturday, and Latchford had rather the best of the game, and eventually ran out winners by five goals to two. For the viiitors, Heald and Dixon, at ha f back, played a fine defensive game, whilst Grounds was the pick ot the etack. The goals were scored by Grounds (2), Spence (2), Banks (1).

, NORTHERN V. STOCKTON' HEATH. Played at litundeUsacds. itebalt :—.Nortbern, eight goals ; Stockton Lleatn, none.

The Possible Electrification of the London and North Western System. Inquiries at Crewe Railway Works on the subject of the possible adoption of electricity as a train motor, instead of steam, on the London and North Western Railway, show that the engineers of the Cambrian Railway have for keveral years had the subject under consideration, and Mr I' W Webb, the chief mechanical engineer,has more than once pub- licly declared that as soon as the directors asked for an electric train the Crewe workmen were ready to turn it out. There is no immediate prob- ability of this, however, thoagh it is recognistd that it is sooner or later inevitable, The laying- down ot the electric plant, however, will be enor- mously costly, though the change would be spread over several years. The North-Western Company are applying ele2cricity to their signalling, and have many electric signals now at work.

The date on which the Northern Union ;representa- tives of Lancashire and Yorkshire meet has not yeh been fixed. The efforts of the Lancashire Management to bring it off on any of the dates they suggested have failed, and now there has been a discussion on the date most recently put forward by the Yorkshire Committee —March 1. This, in the opinion of the Lancashire Com- mittee, expressed at last night's meeting,is not suitable on account of the likelihood of Lancashire and Cheshire making a tie for the Championship, and having regard- to the fact that by March first the Cup-ties would be near. The proposition is that February 15 or Shrove Tuesday be offered to the Yorkshire Committee or, in the event of this being unsitisfactory, any week-day between January 14 and February 15. It is sincerely to be hoped that an arrangement will be come to before the summer. At the present rate of travelling this im- portant match will be played some time, in the next century.

We heartily wish all our readers a Merry Christmas and a Happy New Year.

31EmsEr.s and suppor- I Lord Rosebery's 1 ters of H. M. Govern- 1 Pronouncement. leant have been very insistent in their professed wish that Lord ROSE- BERY should return to a position of influence in the Liberal party. Judging from some of the comments in the Ministerial press, however, these gentlemen are far from grateful at having their wish. Now that his lodship has said what he has to say, and what he is prepared to do in further- ing Liberal principles, it cannot be said that he has been sparing of those who because of his sympathy with them on some points doubtless expected much more lenient treatment at his hands. It is not pleasant for • members of the Government to be told that there is a longer indictment against them than there has been against any Government in Lord ROSEBERY'S recollection. That is the charge that his lordship ma,ces, and he proves it up to the hilt. He shews that the conduct of the Government has led up to 1 the present crisis. Not only has the Colonial Secre- tary's "undoubted power of invective" been a source of inconvenience and even danger in this country, involving it in the hatred and ill-will of the peoples of Europe, but the general tactlessness of the Government in all its dealings was:respon- sible for the war itself, and the Liadequate methods by which it has been waged from the beginning. Before the war began the preparatory arming of the Boers was known to the Govern- ment, although the Paimn MINISTER denied the fact. Knowing of these preparations, the Govern- ment ought to have demanded the occasion of them. Having failed to do so, they ought at least to have guaranteed the Empire against them by a proper supply of materials and adequate men I on the spot for purposes of defence. Had propor precautions been taken, there is little doubt that the history of the war would have been very differ- ent from what it is. The Government made the Jameson Raid the excuse for their inaction. But LORD RossiikkY makes it clear that what tied the hands of the Government was not the Raid,which might have taken place in any well-governed State, but what happened afterwards. The Govern- ment ought to have probed the Raid to the bottom, and paid just compensation for the dis- turbance it caused. Their failing to do so sapped the very foundations of our diplomatic attitude to the Transvaal, and spoilt everything. Not less serious is the point of the indictment regarding the Dissolution and General Election of 1900, than which Lord RoscskitY assures us nothing has struck more deeply at the roots of political moral- ity since 1867. The Dissolution, his lordship tells us, burnt into his mind like an acid, and nothing can wipe out the history of that transaction. He proposes some sort of reparation, which, however, it is doubtful the G3vernment will consent to make, unless, indeed, uader pressure of stronc, necessity arising from quite other circumstances than any attitude of penitence—it is that when the war really does come to an end the Government should set themselves right with the constituencies, and with the world at large, by again appealing to the country at a General Election. Even the oft-repeated taunt that the war has been prolonged by the sympathy 1 expressed by th'e Opposition is matched with the set-oft of the scandalous misrepresentations made by Government supporters during the election, that in addition to the eighty Irish members, whose anti-patriotic sympathies were well known, a large number of Liberal members were returned to represent Boer ideas. So the cant phrase, used by a high authority, that every seat gained by a Liberal was a seat for the Boers, was responsible

===== 0002601_19011220_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
more than anything that had been said on the Opposition benches for encouraging the Boers in their resistance. Thus, whatever may be the final result of Lord ROSEBERVB re-appearance in poli- tics, the hope that it will do anything but damage to the present Government is a vain one and doomed to certain disappointment.

COMMANDANT KRIT- The itzinge Capture of ZINGER having been Kr r. wounded and captured in attempting to break through the line of block- houses leaves the guerilla forces in the east of the Transvaal a rapidly diminishing quantity. It seems that the desperate situation to which his forces were reduced led him to think once more of entering Cape Cc4lony with a small band of intim- ate followers. In this he has failed, having been caught in the attempt, and the scattered irre- concilables now left in that district Will be greatly weakened by his loss. KRITZINGER has been called the Da WET of the Cape,and his long resid- ence in Cape Colony had certainly made him well acquainted with it, especially that hilly portion on the east side, where he had no difficulty in assum- ing the leadership of an irregular force, which he employed with conspicuous skill and no little success. It was to his efforts with this force that the rising in the Colony at one time assumed a serious aspect. About a year ago he crossed the Orange, and found the colonial Dutch in a ripe condition for rebellion, the ground having been carefully prepared previously I under the auspices of the ACricander Bond. The result of this expedition was one of those serious factors in the lengthening out of the war which have not been fully appreciated in this country. Near the border the great majority of the inhabi- tants were Dutch, and in many cases young men did not require a second invitation to shoulder the Mauser and go off with the commando. Even where:the farmers did not take such active part in the operations they rendered efficient assistance to their compatriots in various ways. KRITZINGER knew not only the physical features of the country, but the widespread disaffection among the CapePoers which had been evinced at the ' famous Worcester Conference. He had, therefore, no difficulty in penetrating to within a hundred miles of Capetown, dexterously evading the British troops, who were under the personal command of Lord KITCHENER himself. This, of course, was a matter of no great difficulty when every second farm was an intelligence station as well as a depot for food, forage, and even horses. Last Christmas he had survived two skirmishes with our troops, and at Rosmead Junction captured a train with some Colonials. On February 6th he defeated a squadron of the 7th King's Dragoon Guards and some West Australians, and a few days later he surprised and overwhelmed a Yeomanry patrol near Willow more. In April last he was still giving trouble, and cap- tured some of the 17th Lancers and Yeomanry; and as late as September he led a night surprise on LOVAT'S Scouts, who fought with the utmost de- votion. Latterly he gained an unenviable noto- riety by announcing his determination to put to death all Kaffirs found in the British lines,whether armed or unarmed. Considering the use the Boers had made of natives throughout the war, this de- termination was as unreasonable as it was inhuman ; and as it has been largely practised, it is not improbable that the Commandant who thus initiate A the policy will have to stand his trial in connection with it. His capture is certainly one of the most important incidents of recant British success, and should lead to an early finish to the operations in the eastern districts, where he has flourished so long.

up to 1,425 or over 700 a year. This is altogether irrespective of the large number of cases of burn- ing accidents to young children which do not end fatally, many of which are caused by the clothing of children taking fire at unprotected firegrates. The Secretary of State is justified in calling atten- tion to this matter which has been commented upon again and again in the Press. The remedy is so simple that parents are guilty of culpable negligence when they allow their children to play about the hearth whilst the fire is unprotected by a guard.

Among the many anomalies of the registration law is one which has just been confirmed by the Court of King's Bench. A man who presumably was quite capable of paying his way in ordinary circumstances found himself the victim of that most serious of domestic trials—his wife became insane. In the end he was obliged to send her to the County Asylum, and the expense of her main- ' tenance there was defrayed by the union. He has been struck off the register on the ground that he has received parochial relief. Technically no doubt, this is so; but, regarding the case as typical of the position of an ordinary working- man, the principle is not just. It is like saying that a man who, from circumstances beyond his control, is asked to pay for the support of two homes, and is unable to pay for more than one, shall lose his rights in regard to tne one. The labourer with a family of young children, who is deprived of th 3 co-opera- tion of his wife in rearing their family, has a very hard task; even as a widower he has the chance of finding for them a stepmother. In the other circumstance, when he has to pay for house-clean- ing and cooking, the condition is much harder. Many thousands who enjoy a vote without ques- tion have a much lighter burden of responsibility to the State, and the idea of limiting the franchise is only that its exercise should be confined to re- sponsible citizens. The Registration Law must be thoroughly revised before long, and then other faults may be removed. It is unfair, for instance, if a lodger is to be held entitled to a vote that he should be liable to have to renew his claim in per- son every year. Then, of course, there is the obvious injustice in the qualifying period on removal.

The announcement that Signor Marconi has succeeded in opening communication by wireless telegraghv between the Cornish cost and St John's Newfoundland, has been received with interest in scientific circles, though, perhaps, without the thrill which imaginative correspond- ents on:the other side of the Atlantic predicted. Once wireless telegraphy has been established over a distance of sixty miles, its extension to thousands need cause little surprise. The prin- ciple is the same. It is only a question of sending a more vigorous impulse through the etheric medium, or of providing a mere delicate receiver. It may seem a little ungrateful to say it, but per- haps more good would have been done had the time and expenditure lavished on these experiments been devoted to the removal of diffi- culties which undoubtedly exist when wireless telegraphy is carried on over 'the most moderate distance. In particular the problem of" tuning," by which a receiver may, as it were, p'ck out its own message from scores of others which may be travelling over the same space, has by no means been cleared up, though some advance has been made in this direction. Signor Marconi has heard, it appears, the three faint tans which formed the prearranged signal with the Cornish station, but how far this is even from the degree of success which would enable a long message of the Corona- tion to be sent over sea! How far, too,from any commercial success from such an undertaking, so long as the cables which connect the two conti- nents are intact! Still there is probably a great future before Marconi and his invention. 's

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

" Alas ! how the time rolls on:' So exclaimed Horace ages ago. And so will say and feel many readers before another fortnight passes. It is old- fashioned to moralise, but it is impossible to escape reminders which appear on every hand of the fact that within a week from now the joy bells will have announced the nineteen hundred and first anniversary of the birth of the Prince of Peace; any more than it is to recognise the disturbing fact that the all-important day will find hostilities in progress in South Africa, a similar state of things in the Phillipines, rac at hatred in ihina,and great unrest in three continents of the world. Cynics will point to this'as evidence that the world is not getting better, and the cynics, as usual, will be in ' the wrong. The Millennium may appear to ba a long way off as yet, and there is much strife, not only between nations but between the various sec- tions of society; but the world is not so bad after all, inasmuch as the state of Christendom to-day will contrast very favourably with the condition of things at the end of the first year of the nine- teenth Century; As the years come round the regular festivals are no doubt observed generally in calmer fashion, and to Christmas this especially applies. There is not less warmth, not less genuine goodwill, only the outward form takes different expression. The roast beef and turkey still hold their own as the dishes peculiarly associated with the day, but the village squire does not settle down afterwards to a huge carouse with his cronies, or the young bloods play wild pranks witn all and sundry. The Yule-log burns; the cup of kindness circulates, the old become reminiscent and the young frolicsome, but nothing more. Pleasures peculiar to the season begin earlier now- a-days, with anticipations of presents to give, and of greetings to be received from old friends rarely seen; those pretty messages, so attractive and so universal.

There are many brave relatives or friends in South Africa now looking out for those Christmas tokens, in the shape of a pretty card, which may not be much in itself but is looked upon as show- ing that those at home are still thinking of the absent ones. At thousands of homes on Wednes- day there will be a vacant chair, and at only too many that chair will never more be filled. There will be widows to mourn for their husbands, mothers to weep for sons, and the cry of the fatherless must assuredly add to the wail of sorrow that shall haunt the dreams of Mr Kruger and his allied friends.

There may not be much festivity, in the sense in which it is regarded at home, aroand the camp fires of the veldt; but Tommy is a first class philosopher, and will no doubt extract all the merriment possible, if only in amusement at the contrast between Christmas in his comfortable English cottage and the same season where the hest is so oppressive, while he is chasing the disappearing Boer. There is every prospect, at any rate, that the British soldier will fare much better this season than he I did last in regard to his Christmas dinner. For parents in England are assured by correspondents on the spot. as well as in the order issued by Lord Kitchener, that each soldier shall be presented with one plum-pudding, a quarter pound of tobacco, and a pint of beer, out of the profits of the Field Forces Canteen, and the British public are asked to imagine, if they can, what one hundred and eights -eight thousand plum-paddings will look lil:e! The puddings, beer, and tobacco will cost the Canteen some sixteen thousand pounds; and on the generally accepted priucipls that rewards follow battles, the public will assuredly consider that the British saldier richly deserves all this.

- Post Office authorities are just now exercised over the red envelope which has begun to be issued by stationers and fancy dealers, and has hitherto bea regarded as the exclusive property of the Depart- ment. Much as we ourselves tray be occasionally exercised by the vagaries of Post-Offica officials, having regard to the peculiarly onerous and impor- tant character of duties at Christmas and certain other periods of the year, which they are expected well and diligently to perform, it will be generally recognised that nothing should be done by the public to add to the difficulties of the situation. It appears that this particular envelope was designed f)r war correspondence,and•for sp3cial communica- tions to be called for late at night, or on Sundays. Hence the heads of the slepsrtinent point out the confusion likely to arise from a general use of the red envelope. It would scarcely seem desirable, in the interests of the publiz. to make use of such envelope under the circumstances. In regard to another protest, however, which the authorities have thought proper to issue simultansouds, there will probably be greater difficulty in effecting com- pliance oa the part of those concerned. The way some firms have of covering envelopes with their advertisements must at times be very embarrass- ing to the sorters, and, if continued to the extent it is at present may yet lead to its being prohibited entirely. Advertisers are of ten very unreasonable in this respect,and the cause of much delay and loss of time, when time is really of the first import- ance.

--- If towns which are troubled with the sight of the red rag of Municipal Trading could only be persuaded to admit that there is salvation, in this as in so many other things pushed to ex- tremes, te be found in the adoption of a " golden mean," it would save much of useless and unneces- sary hearthurning. Despite the exhortation of the Tenth Commandment, there are undoubtedly very , many persons who loudly inveigh against all manner of what is called "trading "on the part of local authorities, will yet be disposed to envy the rate- payers of Nottingham their remarkable success in this direction. Last year Nottingham made a pro- fit of £27,000 from gas, £3,000 from tramways, £.5,000 from waterworks, and £6,000 from electric- ity, while at the same time it supplied the town with gas, water, trams, and electricity cheaper than these things are being supplied by trading com- munities in other provincial towns. These profits, moreover, have enabled the municipal council to reduce the rates by a shilling in the

How certain provincia—l—toWns are rated may be shown by the following facts and figures. West Ham, Southampton, and Wakefield have previously been mentioned as paying highly for the privilege of local government. The ratepayers of the first named borough have to pay 93 8d in the pound, while in the other two the sum respectively exacted is Os 61. The • people of Lancaster enjoy the felicity of having to pay the smallest sum on record for the upkeep of their municipal machin- ery and prestige. All expenses in that happy capital are met by a rate of 3s 10J in the pound. The return as to the profits made by municipal trading are interesting and instructive. Notting- ham has been referred to; Liverpool by this means clears no less than £104,323; and Manchester £85,000 annually. The highest poor-rate is paid in East liam, 23 Sid, while Blackpool touches the other extreme with sixpence in the pound. In Eastbourne the lowest school-rate in the country is represented by one farthing, while Chester and I Bury School Boat& exact a lisPpenny each. Curi- ously enough, Southport and Bournemouth appear to have no School Board rate. Of course, the in- ference from an examination of these statistics, is that the seaside resorts are the most lightly rated in this country. Brighton Pays 63 211, but that !n- -eludes the cost of the water supply. The generality of the other popular resorts on the sea escape the burdens of taxation very easily, and the reason, per- haps, is that the visitor contributes with an un- conscious generosity to the revenues of these desirable places.

BYE-LAWS FOR NEW STREETS.

The Local Government Board wrote under date 30th November, stating they had given consideration to the several proposals of the District Council to amend the bvelaws with respect to new streets in force in certain contributory places in the district. The Board observed that these proposals differed in each case, and unless there were special grounds for the difference it appeared to them to be undesirable to require a greater width for back streets in the contributory place of Stockton Heath, than in the contributory place of Delsby, or the contributory place of Frodsham Lordship , etc.

On the propo3ition of Mr Woodward the bye-laws were adopted with the exception of Etnkton Heath.

WARRINGTON LIGHT RAILWAY.

The Clerk repotted receipt of a copy of the applica— tion of the Warrington Corporation to the Light Railway Commissioners for powers to construct a light railway. On the proposition of Messrs Linker and Woodward it was resolved that the matter be left in the hands of the Parliamentary Committee.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.]

This week it is my privilege to wish the readers of the "Examiner " a very " Merrie Christmas.' The atmospheric conditions are seasonable, ii not perhaps what one would desire, but Christmas can be spent very merrily in spite of the drawbacks. ,

A very gratifying feature at the last meeting or the Library Committee was the noticeable desire of the inhabitants to make use of the books at their disposal. Fiction, though it still reigns supreme,t is being real almost bide by side with the more weightier subjects. Two sevenths of the outpa belong to the more solid w)rks and this speaks- well for the discrimination of the townsfolk.

Very properly the Rural Council refused Dr Wood permission to build seats on the roadside for the use of his consumptives at present lodged. in 'the Liverpool sanatorium. The danger of allowing these persons to occupy fixed positions is too ap- parent to need stating and doubtless the inhabit- ants of the adjacent townships will be deeply grate- ful for the unanimous decision of the local I authority.

Mr Naylor pathetically writes from London to the Council asking is it fair that he should be driven from home by a nuisance which has been located? It will be a crumb of comfort for him to learn that certain improvements suggested by Drs Penny and Adams are being carried out. It is to be honed that these will be completed by Christmas Eve so that the worthy gentleman may, at least, spend his Christmas at home free from that most übiquitous, mysterious odour.

Oa a technicality the Urban Council have lost their case against the persons summoned for slaughtering pigs. Those interested in private :1 slaughtering are naturally jubilant at the decision, „J This, however, must not be taken ti mean that per- SOll4 have still the right, by law,to ga on killing at a' the back of their dwelling houses. When the neces- sary advertisements aryl posters have been issued. the point will have to be thrashed out, before the justices. 221

It will be seen that the Runcorn. Urban Council have decided to spend £7OO on new sewers. As pointed out in a letter by a correspondent it would be well if at the same time the local author— ity could see their way to do away with one or two other grievances which exist and particularly about the one to which.our correspondent refers.

- C. H. HAGUE.

Citernm I.

Long Jim they called him on-the station. He, was tall, thin, and stood erect. His-face was plain, and an ugly scar stretched half waracrosslis fore-1 head. Yet his expressionwas kind, though melan— eholy ; and his-eyes. shone with a steady, honest' light. No-one knew his. real.name. He-was regarded as- a kind of mystery, and this rinspired-a feeling of respect. Some.people-affiemed that "'ewes an Earl- in disguise. Undoubtedly,-he---had,been well edu.- • cated, and possessed all the. attributes of a gentle- man. Moreover, he wastievershort of ready-money. Yet he shunned the-society of hia.fellow creatures, and no one dared-question him as .to-his past. He was a man of few words • a.man withn history. Who be was, and whatate,had been, concerned him- self alone. He was now, "Long Jim—the Over- seer." Oncea year be left-the station, and nothing was heard of him for nearly a mouth. Then he returned, and continued his solitary life as before. The salary that was handed to him he took without a word, caring not whether the-amount was large or small : and invariably the money found its way into the pockets of the station hands. When first he came to the station, he knew nothing of the work.he appeared-anxious to engage in. But it so happened that an extra band was badly needed, and he was taken ; even though the manager felt inclined to regard hiin as an " English gentleman loafer." But Long Jim, as he was soon christened, sur- prised everyone. Ile was quick to learn, and not too proud to take advice. Moreover, lie quickly proved himself to be a man in whom could be placed the ftlll4,•st confidence. Faithfully and well'he carried out his duticei: and when in course of time the over- seer left the station to go elsewhere, Long Jim was appointed to fill the vacant position. He proved worthy of the trust reposed in him, and the manager began to esteem him more and more highly. -He felt curious too, regarding Jim's history, and did his best to cultivate the overseer's acquaintance. But Jim repelled all advances, and kept to himself. At his own expense he built a little cottage on the bank of a creek at some distance from the home station, and lived there alone. From early dawn to dark he went about his work. Then, when the night came, he started off to his solitary 'home, and was seen no more until the morning. It was said that through the long winter evenings he sat staring into the tire for hours together, brooding over his past life : and a nervous lad affirmed that. when paasing the cottage one moonlight night, he had heard deep groatui from within. And five years passed. It was growing near the time when Long Jim usually took his annual holi- day. He had been _very busy lately. Two new rooms had been added to his cottage, and a quantity of furniture now arrived from a distant township. The station people opened their eyes and wondered. But Jim said nothing. He left for his holiday, and in a month, returned, bringing With him a wife, and an old woman who he said had nursed his wife in her childhood.

It was a nine days' wonder. One man hinted that the girl who had come back with him was his mis- tress; and Jim overheard him. The man left the station that day. with aching bones, and returned 110 more. And never again was an evil word spoken of Jian's wife.

She wa.s not handsome; but her face was sweet to look upon, and her heart overflowed with love for her husband. In her tastes she was simple ; what pleased Jim—pleased her. She was above all things a womanly woman. One to comfort and console in the time of sorrow and trouble ; one to rejoice with those she loved, in the day of success and triumph. A wife—tender, faithful, and self-sacri- ficing. Her name was a common one—" Janet." Jim called her "Honor," because he said that honor was dearer to him than anything upon earth. They were very happy. When Jim was away at his work, Janet cultivated her garden, or busied her- self about the house. Then in the evenings they took long walks together: or sat before the fire, dreaming dreams and building castles in the air. The shearing was finished. The winter had been a dry mad, and there appeared every prospect of a drought. "We must get rid of some sheep," said the manager; for the run was overstocked. Some- one must take them to the nearest mazket. and the journey would be long and difficult, for feed and water were scarce. Only one man could be relied upon to take charge of the job ; and this was Long Jim. He was chosen.

Jim had been married some six months, and he shrank from the thought of parting from his wife. Yet he had never been one to shirk Ms duty: and the day drew near for him to leave on his journey. Time drags heavily t.o some; to Jim it flew apace. The evening was chilly, and Long Jim and his wife sat hand in hand before the fire. On the morrow Janet would lose the man she loved, and who loved her in return. Both were silent, for the thought of parting weighed heavily upon then,. And Janet leaned her bead on Jim's shoulder, and gazed at the glowing coals through a mist of tears : but she did not sob, and she spoke no word. And presently- Jim stud —" Honor."

She scatted, slid clasped her arms about his neck. "What is it, Jim ? " she asked brokenly. Have you ever regretted that you married me l"' "You know that I have not." "You have never questioned me concerning my life before I met you. Do you ever wonder who and what I am ? "

"Sometimes, Jim. But you told me there was a secret connected with your past that must go with you to the grave, and I have never wished to know it. So long as I have your love, I am content. That scene; best to you, my husband, is right and best in my eyes also.

"And ,-ou trust me, Honor ? " She looked into his face with a sweet smile. "If all the world believed ill of you, and you told me the world was right, my faith in you would not be shaken. I could believe anything from your lips, save that you had wronged a fellow creature." Jim kissed her passionately. "Thank God," he murmured, "I have lived to know a good and a pure woman."

And then they spoke of that journey which both dreaded. It would take several months to accom- plish, and Janet would be very lonely with only her old nurse to keep her company. But she clung to Jim, assuring him that'she would be very brave, and would not fret during his absence. "You will be horns for Christmas, won't you Jim ? " she said wistfully.

" Yes," he anwered. "Nothing shall keep me from you on Christmas Day." And then, trying to cheer her, he said What am I to bring you for a Christmas Box, Honor ? "

" Yourself, Jim; that is all I want." Oh !-but that won't do. You must choose something else as well." _ _ _

"Have your portrait taken, and that will be the nextbest thing to having you." "A good idea," exclaimed Jim. " I shall take your photo with me, and have two small copies made, one of yours, and another of mine. Then I shall have thorn placed in a gold locket; and there I you will have your Christmas Box." "You won't forget, Jim F About your own, I mean. It doesn't matter about mine."

"I think differently," said Jim, with a smile. Janet nestled closer to him. " I shall have a Christmas Box for you too, Jim, when you come home," she said in a low voice. "And what is it to be ? be asked gaily. But he hid her face on his shoulder, and would not answer. Then Jim took her face in his hands and compelled her to meet his gaze. Her eyes ;hone with a strange light, and her cheeks blushed crim- son. In a moment Tim understood. He clasped his wife in his arms with a hoarse err. "I cannot leave you, Honor," he exclaimed. " It is impossible— impossible." "Hush, Jim," said Janet soothingly. " There is none to take your plane, so that you nitt.o: go. Have no fear for me, all will be well, my husband. Keep up a brave licazt. for my sake, Jim." "But it seems so terrible that I should be away when—when—"

"I would rather have you here, bat it cannot-he, therefore we must think no more about it. Believe me, Jim, the future has no terrors for me: as I said. —all will be well ; something within me tells me. that tbia is 80. Come, Jim, let us only look forward tu haw re•uoion." All night long, Jim lay with wide open -era; sjpg into therclarknes.s. Janet slept fitfully; but when the dim dawn began to break, her sleep-he-, came heavy and tranquil. Then Jim rose softly, 4lnd dressed himself. Stooping over the bed, be kissed the fair hair of his wife with yearning tea— derneen. A moment later he had leftthe cottage, and was heading towards the iltstant south, his face Pet and stern. Strong man though he was, las shrank from the pain of a parting scene.

'I HE EXAMINE_R FRIDAY, DECEMBER 20 1901

Cuarran, Christmas-morning-broke bright and clear. With the exception oPthe -wreck of-Loug Jim's cottage. and the loss of-eome of-the stock, no very serious damage had been wrought"hrtheetonii-Old.Rachel, the nurse, seemed lICIDO the werselor thedright ; and Janet too, despitedier-weak•statsof -health, was slowly recovering. It was-at, first feared that the shock might prove tooanneh-forter,; butehe had a brave heart, anchstroug in the -Imowledge that Jim had returned, sheallovreckno thought of the previous night's-adventure to-trouble her. She and-Jim were- together this-Christmas morn- kg... Jim had been provided with atiuit of the manager's clothes, and -Janet was laughing at the ludicrous appearance he presented, for the-manager and his overseer differed widely both in height and build. But Jim did.not...raind this in the least; he scarcely took hiseyesoffthe baby which was sleep- ing in Janet's arms, and-the look onappiness upon his face was unmistakeable. Soon, however, his face clouded and he turnednway. "What is it, Jim,f" asked Janet faintly. "I was wondering -what might have happened if —if—if I had not gotbaek wheal did." "You must not think of that, dear. It is ail. over now, and we are safe and sound. Certainly our poor-cottage-has notbeen fairly treated, and—" "That is nothing," exclaimed Jim ; "but it seems incredible -to me that no one on the station thought of going to your assistance. I cannot understand it."

Yon are unjust, Jim. It was impossible for anyone to venture out during the storm; and who was to know that the creek bad risen so high as to flood thecottage ? Besides, at such a time, the men had to think of Themselves; and remember how kind Mr. Leighton has been to us, Jim." "Rachel should have gone for help," muttered Jim.

" She wished to do so; but the flood came upon us so suddenly; and—and—l couldn't bear the thought of being left alone. You see, dear, I was ill, and your absence was worrying me." "Poor girl," said Jim, kneeling by the bedside, and taking her hand. "Let us try and ferget it, Honor, and speak of other things. lam afraid our Christmas will hardly be a merry one; but at least we can be happy. And no, dear, here is the locket I promised you. You see I have not forgotten it !" Janet took it with a sweet smile of thanks. " How pretty it is," she exclaimed. "And, oh, what a good portrait of you, Jim. lam glad you had it taken."

"Then show your gia.titude by going off to sleep," said *Slim, as he kissed her forehead. "I was forgetting that you ought not to be worried. No more talking, mind." "When did your presence ever worry me ?" asked Janet brightly. "Never, I hope. Still, it is better that I should go.'' But Janet called him back.

"I have something to tell your, Jim," she said. What is it, dear?" _ _ _

"About a week ago, Mr. Leighton left two letters at the cottage, which he said were to be delivered to you on your return. I should have mentioned them last night perhaps, but you know I was not capable of thinking about anything then." " I don't suppose they contained much of im- portance," remarked Jim carelessly. " One of the envelopes was edged with black; and—and the letters were not addressed as they usually are, dear."_

, -- Jim started. " How were they addressed, then i" he asked.

"To Sir Philip Fordham, Bart.," was Janet's reply. For a moment Jim said nothing. Then he asked, with assumed calmness—" What did you do with them ?"

"I told Rachel to place them in the recess with your other papers, Jim." "They have probably been carried away with the flood; but at any rate I may as well see if I can find some trace of them. And in the meantime, Honor, get all the sleep you can." Jim's face worked strangely as he left the house, and strode off in the direction of his cottage. He scarcely noticed the Christmas greetings of the station hands he met, nor did he reply to their in- quiries concerning his wife. His eyes were bent on the ground; he appeared buried in thought. " What does it mean ?" he asked himself. " Surely Walter cannot be dead ! But if so—did he first confess ? If not —can I return to England? No, no; it is im• possible—impossible." He walked quickly, and soon arrived at his des- tination. The flood had subsided; there was now no difficulty in entering the cottage, and Jim at once went to the recess in which his letters had been placed. This recess had been built into the wall by Jim's instructions, and the water had barely reached it. It was here that he kept a number of unim- portant papers; but here too were the letters he had come to seek, dry and wholly uninjured. Without even glancing at the sodden carpets and the dismal sight his home presented, he secured the letters and left the cottage. But as he passed through what once had been a garden, he murmured Poor Janet. She was very proud of her flowers. This will be a sad blow to her.

Going on a little distance, he came to a large gum. One of the branches had long ago been broken off by a strong wind, and on this Jim seated himself, and prepared to peruse his letters. First he broke the seal of the smaller envelope, and read and re-read the contents. Then he transferred his attention to the other, which proved to be a legal looking docu- ment. For over an hoqr he sat there, occasionally glancing at the papers in his hand, but for the greater part of the time, staring fixedly in front of him.

At length he rose, and walked slowly towards the station. On the way he met the manager, whc in hearty tones wished bun a merry Christmas. Jim thanked him almost mechanically, and was about to move on, but Mr. Leighton said, enquiringly : " Been having a look at the cottage, I suppose Rather in a mess, eh I" " Yes—at least, I hardly noticed it." " Did you get your letters, or have they gone down the creek ?"

" Thanks—l have them." Then, struck by a sudden thought, Jim asked, "How came you to conclude that they were for me, Mr. Leighton ?" " I received a note from a firm of English solici- tors, in which I was asked to forward the letters on to James Severn, if by any chance that gentleman had left the station. Fortunately I was expecting you back almost daily ; and seemingly you arrived just in time. Lady Forclhain has had a very un- pleasant experience, but niy wife tells me that she bears up splendidly." The unusual title caused Jim to stare ; but seeing a merry twinkle in the manager's eyes, he smiled good-humouredly. "I am very thankful to say that she seems little the worse for her fright, Mr. Leighton." " Well—well ; I see I am about to lose the best overseer I ever had, but it can't be helped. Good- bye for the present, Sir Philip ; no doubt you are anxious to get back to your wife, but we must have a long talk when you are at liberty." Long Jim continued his walk towards the Man- ager's house, as if in a dream. Janet was sleeping as he entered her room, but she woke almost imme- diately, and glanced up at him, startled. " What is it, Jim ? How strange you look." " Do I ?" he answered.

_ - - " Have you had bad news, dear? Tell me—what is it ?"

"Are you sure you are strong enough to listen to a rather long story, Honor ?" "Yea—if it. concerns you, my husband." ' Seating himself by the bedside, Jim took Janet's hand in his own, and at once began his story. "You will remember my telling you that there was a secret connected with my past which must go with me to the grave. Something, however, has oc- eurred which renders it unnecessary for me to keep silent any longer, and it is only right that you should learn why I have lived for so long under an assumed name; and why I have kept you so much in the dark regarding my past history." "Believe me, Jim—l ant content to remain in Ignorance, if you would rather it should bo so," said janet earnestly._

" I know that well, Honor, and the thought of your faith in your husband is very sweet to me. But the time has now come when the story- of my life should be made known to you. My real name is Fordham. .This I mentioned to you on our wedding day, when I told you also that, for reasons of my own, had assumed the name of James Sev- ern. :ma would retain it till my death. My father —Sir Walter Fardha in --came of a very old family, :snd wag highly respected. He scorned all things mean and underhand. ; loved truth and justice ; was extremely proud ; and unfortunately—very het tempered. He bad two 80118. I was the younger, my brother, called Walter after his father, being throe years my senior. In appearance we were much alike, lout not in disposition. Walter was full of life and fun ; I was rather inclined to be melancholy. My father almost worshipped his eldest born ; he eared nothing for me, and I cannot blame him. Through ute he lost his wife, for she died in giving ine.birth. The meaning of a mother's love was un- known to me, and my childhood was not a happy; one. I looked up to my brother as to a hero, and I loved him passionately. Once, when a child, I wasl in danger of drowning, and he saved my life. Froml that motu_ent_l btoane lait3 gan,.. 'What fo.lloverl

ann rwrn not dwelrlong upona:- brother went into the army, whilst I rernainedsit home, and made myself useful on the estate. Fora thne-all went well; then Walter, who was always a-trite wild, got mixed up with a fast set, and took Laganasling and drinking. This led him heavily into debt. and-he applied to me for help. I gave him alLthe money I had, which was very little, as my Lather made.me but a small allowance. Still, with thia.assistance, Walter was able to meet his most pressins* engagements; but unfortunately his mania for-gunibling grew stronger and stronger. At length he wrote, begging me to meet him in London on a certain day, as he needed my help. I went and found him in-despair. He had again got into-diffi- culties, and unless £l,OOO could be raised immediate- lhis would be dishonoured. My father, of course, knew nothing of Walter's weakness and folly • and it seemed to me that his best plan would beatolaake.a clean breast of everything. Yet when I advised him to pursue this course, herefused angrily, and accused me of wishing to usurp his place-in-Sir Walter's affections. We parted, and I hard nothing of him for some time, though I remained-in London, hoping that he would come to seethe advisability of following my advice; and thiaking also that I might prove of some use to him. One night—l shall never forget it—he came to mrhotel. His face was haggard, and he appeared utterly reckless. Without waiting for me to ques- tion him, he told me that he had forged a cheque on Sir Walter for a large amount. This cheque he bad presented to the Bank, and it had been honoured. A little later the forgery had been detected; the matter was in the hands of the police, and Sir Wal- ter himself was in the city. My brother told me also that the clerk who had cashed the cheque had sup- plied the police with a lifelike description of the man who had presented it. I was the only person who knew of my brother's monetary difficulties, and he entreated me to remain silent concerning them, and to do my best to hide his guilt. I loved him, and ,gave the promise readily enough. The next moment soy father was announced. He was surprised-to see Walter with me, and also seemed to experience a certain amount of grim pleasure at finding us to- gether. So much the better,' he said; it is well that Walter should see his brother in his true light.' Then he turned to me, saying harshly, I have here a cheque bearing my forged signature. It was presented at my bank by a man who strongly re- sembles you, and who succeeded in obtaining cash for it. Kindly inform use whether this is your signature.' He banded the cheque to me, and pointed to the endorsement. There was my name staring me in the face--Philip Fordham. "in a moment all was clear to me. Walter, in addition to forging his father's signature, had secured his own safety by endorsing the-cheque in my name. The room swam round with me, and I stag- gered. But my father's voice -restored my presence ef,mind. Can you explain this rhe demanded sternly. I was silent. I remembered the promise Fired given to Walter but a few-minutes before; I realised that my father loved his list born passion- ately, and knew that the discovery of Walter's guilt would break his heart. Moreover, I too loved my brother, and owed my life to him. Sir Walter re- peated--his question, and I answered—' I have no explanation to offer.' This enraged him, and hard words were spoken. Yet I remained silent, and my silence exasperated him still further. Losing all self control, he raised his stick and struck me across the forehead. I fell, stunned and bleeding. When I recovered consciousness my father VMS bending over me, wiping away the blood with his handker- chief. Finding that I was not seriously hurt be took his leave ; and Walter, without a word to me, went with him. I never saw either of them again, and I have never lost the sear caused by that blow. An attempt was made to hush up the whole affair; but somehow the story of the forgery got abroad. I left England, came to Australia, and as James Severn, have lived here ever since. That, Honor, is the story of my life." _ _ _

A whole world of love and pride shone in Janet's eyes, as she looked up at her husband. Pressing his hand tightly she whispered—" It was very noble of you, Jim, to act as you did.. But your brother—oh I what a coward be must have been to let you bear thc punishment for his sin I" " Hush, dear," said Jim gently. "He was weak —that is , azd now—he is dead. One of the letters I received this morning was from a clergy- man, who supplied me with the melancholy details of this event. You must know that when I came out here, my father made me an allowance of £l5O a year. He died soon after; and Walter, of course, succeeded to the title and estates; but my allow- ance was left to me by will. Walter married, and his wife' bore him a son; but mother and son were killed on the same day in a railway accident. After this, my brother seems to have become reckless and ruining his health in dissipation and excess of all kinds,his life was breught to an early close. On his death-bed he confessed his crime, and the written confession has been forwarded to me. I have also received a letter from the family solicitors, inform- ing me that I have succeeded to the Baronetcy and estates; and expressing the hope that I will proceed to England as early as possible to settle matters of importance." "You—you must go to England ?" gasped Janet. " Yes ; it will be necessary 0 'clear my name, and of course there are business matters to arrange." "What—what will you do with me, Jim f" "Do with you ?" lie echoed blankly. " Yes; everything seems so strange to me. You see, you—you are rich now—a Baronet; and will have so many great friends who will' look down upon use. I—l am not—" - - - -

- - " You are my wife," said Jim tenderly, "and fit to.rank among the noblest women of the world. I love you, and in my eyes there is no woman on earth your equal. In my dark hours, you were my com- forter ; all my hopes are centred in you. The dark hours have rolled away now, Honor; but if your faith in me is to go with them, then I would rather a thousand times give up my name, my riches, and all that I posisess, and remain 'Long Jim, the over- seer.' " _ .

"My faith in you can never die, you know that, dear; and I shall never cease to love you. But I doubt myself—my fitness—" "Doubt no longer, my darling. Though there have been many Lady Forclhams, none have been more worthy to bear the.titlethan you, Honor." Sle gave him a look, full of gratitude, and for a time was silent. Presently she said, half shyly : " I suppoee I mustn't oall-ru Jim in future ?" "To you lam Jim always, -he answered smiling. " I have grown used-to it and no other name must come front your bps." "I -am glad of that, dear, for I too have grown useiPto the name, and have learned to use it because of him who bears it." And then for the first •timo..lins, as we shall call him also, ventured-to take the,taby in his arms. He felt very awkward, and .appeared amazed at its smallness and lightness. Every moment be felt afraid that the little bundle of snowy drapery would slip through, his fingers, and anxiety was plainly visible in his features. "What an eventful Christmas it has been, Jim," she said. "For Tears you-have-suffered unjustly— ancbsiow your name is cleared; you have come into riches, and have risen from an overseer to be z: Baronet." "Yes," answered ...Tim. "But, best of all, my wife-has been saved from a dreadful death, and 'I have received from her hands —our child—her Christ. was Box." * • * * * * * •

Many years have passed s:nce that Christmas Day, and Long Jim's Christmas, Box is now a man. Sir Philip Fordhant has growsa grey, and his face is much lined; yet he and Ins wife, Janet, together with their eldest son, are_shortly to visit Australia, SO that the future Baronet-may see the home which gave him -birth. A:fierce sun shot its relentless rays.Atpon.ooths parched and arid earth. Not a blade-,of Trass oconid-be-seeneand the sheep lay -clyingirehtuidreds ishonb-therTuti. Above the heavens--blue--ana immeasurable .; in their midst that glaring-,n- ing-, orb-of the day. Around—desolation,-- despairs and-death.

.Awtty+to-thesoirtli, three -dark--,specks -rosanabore are horizon, and came slowly,onward. Horsemen they were, and Long Jim rode in :tlie...lel/1 His mission-was accomplished, thoughathadftakeniong• andbewas returning to hislome.....To-morrow,would befiChristmas Day. The -horses, with hanging heads stusabled -wearily along. Now and again the horsemen—would ffis- mount, and walk mile after .milezto easegtheirjaded 'Weeds. Then once more ther-ovouldget.listlessivinto the,saddle, and push on towards theetationeatill manyrtniles away. The longest day must have- an.end,..andPthe.enn sankdower and-lower in the eky. The.winddropped, wiel:wa.,encfreded by an ominous calm. The air grey/ cooler, and the horses raised their headaand quickened-their pace. "Please-God, me-elrall be. home,for. Christmas Bye,"' said -Bong Jim,..breaking the-silence. "-And right glad the-wifo will be to see-yon," exclaimed one of the men heartily. But the other saidmothing ; only stared anxiously ahead, and. clutched his bridle- rein with neryousting,ers. The Joru-ney-hadbroken down his health, and he feared. something, yet knew not what. It seemed. as though the dread of impending disaster -were-strong within him.

But Long Jim was cheerful, •almost gay. At timeahe broke into snatches ofx;ong, and his com- panions stared in amazement; for the overseer was usually silent, grave and self-contained. To-day, however, a change hall come over him. The intoler- able thirst which for hours hail consumed him, was now forgotten, and he heeded not the glare of the sun—the heat—the dust—or the many otherdiscom- forts of that miserable journey. What mattered it to him that not a drop of water remained in the water- bags? To-night he would be at home ; and which, ever way he gazed, he saw—not the barrea run, the dying sheep, the hovering crows; but a sweet, pure face—the face of Janet—the face of his wife. He laughed to himself; he took from his-breast pocket a. small case, and as he pressed tho- spring, it flew open, and the rays oF the sun flashed upou the gold locket it contained. This was Janet's present; Janet's Christmas box. Jim opened the locket, and gs.zed tenderly at the portrait of his wife. Then he closed it again, and laid it reverently in its case, and returned the case to his pocket. Many times he did this; and he remembered too that Janet had pro- mised hint a Christmas Box. Had ail gone well with her? he wondered. His heart beat rapidly, and lie grew anxious. With every mile, his longing to reach his home increased.

Evening approached. The sunset—red and angry looking. In the northern sky, clouds began to gather thick and fast. The rumble of distant thunder reached the ear; the lightning played hrfitful flashes. "The drought is breaking up, cried Jim; and he pushed forward with his two companions, into the teeth of the advancing storm. Swiftly the heavens were darkened. Great banks of cloud rolled majestically overhead, and the earth was enveloped in a pall as of the deepest night. Sud- dealy the vault of heaven seemed to burst into a sheet of flame, and vivid forks of light darted across the sky. The horses reared, and trembled uith fear. Again all was dark. Then came a sound as of the t roar of heavy cannon on the field of battle. Crash after crash—peal upon peal—shook the earth. The maddened horses plunged forward, and were almost unmanageable, but a few soothing words from their riders—a gentle patting on the neck, and they pressed swiftly onwards. "'We must make for the shepherd's old hut," shouted one of the men. It is only about a mile to the left."

"The station is not more than six miles off, and I mean to go on," answered Jim grimly. What was that strange, weird sound? Like the whirring of many wings it was; the rain falling heavily and rapidly through the air. Soon it was 'upon them ; a perfect deluge, before which they bowed their beads and gasped for breath. To reach the station would be impossible. They must take refuge in the shepherd's hut. Through the black darkness, relieved at intervals by the blinding gla:e of the lightning ; through that never-ending wall of water, the deafening crash of the thunder almost stunning them—they made their way. _ _ _

And at length the goal was reached. The horses were left on the sheltered side of the hut, and hobbled. The men were thoughtful of their steeds, removed their saddles, and carried them into the hut. Then they seated themselves on a rude bench and waited for the storm to pass. _

One long weary hour ran its course, and still there was no change. Flash succeeded flash, vivid, dazzling, almost blinding. The rumbling and crash- ing of the thunder continued unabated; the rain poured down unceasingly. And now, above the storm could be heard a roar as of angry billows beat- ing upon a rocky coast. Long Jim started to his feet, and stared anxiously into the right. A vivid flash of lightning lit up the scene for miles around, and revealed a sight that caused Long Jim to stag- ger. " Good God," he cried. "The river is corn- ing down a banker. I have no time to lose ; I must get home." ' But the others held him back. "It is madness," they exclaimed. "You must wait. The storm can't last much longer; and your cottage is too high on the bank to be in danger." Jim sank down with a groan, and buried his face in his hands. He realised how helpless he was; it was useless struggling against the storm. He thought of his wife alone, save for the presence of an old woman who would only hen hindrance if danger threatened—and the thought maddened him. He rushed to the door of the hut; only to be driven back before the fury of the elements. Nothing could be done ; he must wait, and hope for the best. Slowly the minutes passed, yet, the storm eon- tinned; and at length Jim would remain iialonger. "lam going," he said abruptly ; " you oars stay here, or accompany me, whichever you please." -

There was a moment's pause, then the -men an- swered—" We will go -with you ; but it's adool's game for all that." The horseswere quickly,smldled and started off at a good pace, as though anxious to make up for lost time. The rest had-refreshed them, and their instinct told them that the journey was nearly ended. For the first few miles not a word was spoken. Indeed, it was almost impossible to make one's self heard. But now there came a strange lull. Suddenly the storm had burst upon them, and as suddenly it had passed away. Already the sound of the thunder Was growing fainter and fainter, and the rain had ceased. Long Jim lifted his head and gazed about him. The sky overhead, and to the north, was bright with stars. In the Routh, the heavens were black, and the storm was raging fiercely. A full moon had risen above the distant hilltops, and the scene looked weird and ghostly in the extreme. Their course lay alows, the bank of the creek, and what but a little -while ago had been a dry, sandy bed, was now a foaming, raging torrent. A loud, sullen, continu- ous roaring: sound filled the air; strange, dark objects floated swiftly along with the current; huge logs of wood—the branches of trees—the carcases of sheep that bad been drowned. Long Jim dashed his spurs into the horse's flanks, for a sensation of sickening dread had laid hold upon him. The sweat stood in heaths upon his forehead, and his breath came in short sudden gasps. That ride, brief though it was, seemed to occupy an eternity. But now it was over. He had .reached his :borne. _ _ .

A dim light shone through the window of Janet's room. Round the cottage itself the flood waters whirled —a mass of foam, upon which the moonbeams fell with weird effect. For one brief moment, Long Jim seemed paralysed with fear. Then be sprang from m thehis lips. u saddle, Into the w n da great Neray of anguish burst sto of waters he aushed, and nothing could stay his progress. He entered the house, and made his way to his wife's room, the water reaching above his waist. Here a strange sight met his gaze. On the mantel-piece was a candle, dimly burning ; chairs and other articles floated all about the room, and the bed, though high, was completely under water. And standing on the bed was Janet. She was ill and helpless; yet, assisted by her old nurse, who stood beside her, she had raised herself up, and wac leaning against the wall, her child, hardly more thar fortnight old, clasped in her arms. As Jim rushed to her, she uttered his name ; then closed her eyes. and knew no ruore. -

Very tenderly Long Jim bore his .wife through the raging Head to a Place of safety ; while one-of his companions who had followed close behind, went to the rescue of Janet's nurse. Once more they mounted their horses, and with their helpless bur- dens, pushed on to the house of the manager. _

SANKEY-STREET, Warrington.—(A4vt Sudden Death in Buttermarket-street. On Saturday evening the death occurred with painful suddenness of Miss Polly Balshaw, a resi- dent of Winwick, and who is related to Mr George Howard, metal broker, Hall-street. It appears that deceased was walking along Buttermarket-street, when she staggered and fell to the groud. N.S.P.C.C. Ball.

In aid of the funds of the Warrington and dis- trict branch of the National Society for the Pre- vention of Cruelty to Children, a public ball was held on Tuesday evening in the assembly room at the Lion Hotel. The ballroom had been tastefully decorated for the occasion by Mr Ralph Johnson, and the music for dancing, which was kept up with spirit from nine till 1.30, was efficiently supplied by Mr R H Pearson's band. The programme was an excellent one. The catering was in the capable hands of Mrs Gilbert, manageress of the hotel Major Buckton, Bruche Hall, ably officiated as hon. secretary and treasurer of the ball, and the local Executive Committee of the Society consists of Mrs James Fairclough, Mrs Frost, Mrs Harrison, Mrs Long, Mrs Munro, Mips Appleton, Miss Broadbent, Miss Massey, Mr J D Buckton, Mr V B Davies, Mr A II Lee (hon. secretary) and Mr J McGregor. The ball passed off successfully, the attendance being satisfactory. and all the arrangements were most admirable.

The Crewe Accountantship. At a meeting ot the Crewe Town Council on Tues- day Mr CH Pedley said that the Finance Com- mittee had received a large number of applications for the position of Borough Accountant, and they recommended the Council to appoint Mr W Boyle, of Warrington, at a salary of £2OO a year. As the Corporation accounts now comprise a number of items in the way of electricity charges, and in view of the application for Crewe to become a County Borough in the not far distant future, the appoint- ment of Mr Boyle was most important.—Mr Man- ning asked who was respontible for placing the limit of age of candidates at 33? The Council had taken exception to a company which had fixed the age of their workmen at 40, and here they were s•-tting an even worse example.—Mr C 11 Pedley said toe limit was affixed by the Finance Com • mittee. and the Council approved of it at their last meeting. He dared say the feeling of the committee was that they wanted a fir,st-class man and they could only get a younj man for the salary they 1 offered.—The motion was agreed to.

ST. JOHN'S PRESBYTEaIAN CHURCH WARRINGTON.

Sale of Work. The schoolroom attached to St. John's Presbyterian Church presented a bright and cheerful appearance gis Friday afternoon, when a sale of work in aid of the g_Prigregational funds was opened. The room had been gecorated with a liberal display of bunting, and severs, Stalls had been tastefully arranged with a large an varied assortment of useful and ornamental articles. I addition there was an attractive refreshment stall. Tti etallholders were as follows :—No 1 stall : Mrs Milne _Mrs Kirkpatrick, and Mrs J McDowell ; No. 2 stall Mfrs W McDowell, Mrs Newman, and Mrs Ferguson vefreshment stall : Miss Barlow and Mrs Miller ; bran .111). Mrs Rees. The onerous duties of hen, secretary Were faithfully discharged by Sergeant Williamson. The opening ceremony commeeced at three o'clock was performed by Mrs Macdonald,of Bowdon. THE NORTHERN UNION.

NORTHERN UNION COUNTY CHAMPIONSHIP.

CUMBERLAND v. CHESHIRE. These teams met at Whitehaven on Saturday, before a fairly large attendance. On the Cheshire side Houghton, who was injured in the Yorkshire match, was unable to play, his place being taken by Wilkinson, of Runcorn, On the Cumberland side Nelson (Aspatria) was succeeded at half back by J J Fletcher, (Seaton), and forward Steel (Aspatria) and J Atkinson (Etillom) gave way to Elliott (Aspatria) and Hogarth (Millem). The ground was in a wet condition, and at intervals during the game there were slight falls of snow. Cumberland won the toss, and Farmer started for Cheshire against a slight wind. From the outset the Cheshire backs showed distinct superiority, and good play by Jolley and Richardson compelled Fitzsimmons, for Cumberland, to kick dead twice in quick succession. Robinson attempted to drop a goal, but the ball was charged. Immediately after: wards Farmer attempted to place a goal from a mark, but failed. Subsequent play fluctuated between the Cheshire and Cumberland twenty-fives, and was of an exciting char- acter, but neither side scored up to half-time In the second half Robinson had the ball passed to him by Jol:ey, who passed to Warder, and Ihat player scored a try,Farmer failing at goal. Jorey afterwards got the ball from a scrum- mage near the line.eluded Whitehead. and scored nearithe corner,Farmer again failing at goal. Lightfoot then 'scored a somewhat lucky try,and Farmer kicked a goal Jolley subse- quently scored his second try. Farmer failing at goal. Result s—Cheshire, one goal and four tries (14 points) ; Cumberland, nothing.
