THE NORTHERN LEAGUE.

WARRINGTON BEAT OLDHAM. A GAME IN THE SNOW.

Warrington scored a notable victory on Saturday on the Waters heddings ground where they beat the Oldhamites be five points to two and secured a couple of chalks in the championship table. The severity of the recent atom had been felt with greater force at Oldham than in Warrington and when the 2JO Warrington enthral:lets who followed the Warrington' team to Watersheddings arrived:in the hilly town they were suprised to find it wrapped in a mantle of snow and the pressmen were still more surprised to hear that com- munication with the outside world by telephone was practi- cally nil. About live thousand spectators lined the snow covered field, many of the Oldhamites being induced to attend by the appearance in the three-quarter line of a new player of the name of Hinge, the exaumberston and Keigbley youth, Punctually to time the teams turned out as follows:

-- Warrington : Hallam, back; Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son and Harris. threequarters ; Townsou and Allen half- backs ; Taylor, Fell, Edmondson, Eden, Morrison, Board- man, J Swift and Ashton, forwards. Oldham : Thomas, back; Spottlawoode, S Lee,Hings and S Williams, threequarters ; A Lee and Lawton, half-backs ; Ferguson, Topham,Wilkinson (G), Wilkinson (J H),Bonser, Vowles, Teller and Browne, forwards. Winning the toss, Isherwood wisely decided to take advan- tage of the stiff breeze which was blowing from goal to goal. Ferguson started operations and the new player distin- guished himself at the outset by tackling one of his own side, a Mete performance which drew from the Oldhamites a somewhat sickly smile. From the first scrimmage Town- son got possession and passed out to Dickenson but the last- named player's pass went wide and a good opening was lost. Scrimmaging ensued] on the Oldham line, and the home team were penalised, Lawton playing off side round the scrimmage. For this Werrington were awarded a free kick close to the Oldham line, and Fish had no difficulty in placing a goal. This early success acted like a tonic on the Warrington men and they were soon making matters warm for Oldham in their own twenty-five. Frater brought relief by a dashing dribble in which the Oldham forwards ably seeonded him. leherwcod carried the ball bock with a vigorous run through his opponents and a moment later Dickenson got past Hinge and kicked across the goal. Spottiswoode followed by Fish made a dash for possession and Spottitowoode just succeeded in kicking dead. The drop out brought little relief to Oldham. and Morrison was to the frost in a smart follow uo. Oldham were again penalised and Nish made another attempt to negotiate the ball between the posts but failed. The Old- ham forwards followed up with great dasl'iland headed an invasion into Warrington territory. Hallam cleared them out and after Lees had made a mark, which availed nothing, the ball was corded to the Oldham end, where tall kicking be the Warrington backs ended in nothing more tangible than a dead ball, the wind carrying the oval to the rails. A round of passing by Allen. Townson, and Dickenson placed the home line in danger, and Thomas touched d nen to save. Oldham were defending well, but the Warrington backs corn- bleed beautifully, and Harris getting possession had almost thrust himself over the line when he was knoexed into touch at the corner ilag.Success came shortly afterwards, for a long Pass by Allen placed the ball near Fish and that player pie k ing up, rounded Williams 'and scored what proved to be the only try of the match. Hallam took the kick and made a good attempt to place a goal but failed. The Oldham backs who up to now had been clearly outclassed gave a taste of their quality and S. Less and Wllliams were prominent with geed work. l'elakiag full tile of the wind, War- rington soon changed the venue of operations to the other end and Dickenson made a desperate effort to get over the line on his own account. Harris followed his example at the next opportuoity but he wan collared by Hings. Hallam next destinguished himself by a lofty kick and follow tio. I He was successful in getting possession again and ere he I was collared by Thomas he threw across to Fish and that player was held up on the line by Spottiswoode. Half-time arrived with Warrington leading by five points to nil. Swift restarted and the ball rolled into touch. From the I drop out Allen made a mark. A foi ward dribble by the War- ' rington men took play to the Oldham 25. Here the Warring- ton men were penellsed, and Oldham transferred play to the other end, Hallam being collared with his charge. Play eettleS down in the Warrington 25, but the Oldham backs did not combine well and keen tackling by the Warrington backs kept them out. Isherwood saved finely amidst a host of opponents and Edmondson gave Warrington relief by making a mark. At length Allen was penalised for an infringement in putting the ball into the scrum, and Ferguson made an attempt to kick a goal but failed. A Lees next made a futile attempt to kick a goal.but at last Oldham opened the Score in the same way as Warrington had done. Allen was again penalised and Ferguson succeeded on this occasion In kicking a goal. Oldham now strove hard to get the additional points neededlto win but the Warrington men showed equal determination 10 keep them out. Harris raced away along the touch line to the Oldham twenty-five but the gale which was blowing stopped Warrington from mak- ing much headway. Ferguson made a fraitless attempt te kick another goal. Hallam defended splendidly. Townson tricked his opponent cleverly and got away smartly. Fish followed him up and Townson threw across to him but the wind blew the ball over Fish's head and the opening was lost. Oldham now pressed to the close but they were un- able to pierce the Warrington defence and the visitors re- tired victors of a hard fought game by five points to two.

A CREDITABLEVICTORYFOR WARRINGTON "A."

There ries a good attendance of spectaters at the Wilder- spool enclosure on Saturday to witness the encounter bet ween the local "A " team and Oldham " A." Warrington won (be toss and toe visitors started operations against a stiff breeze. An incursion was made into the War. ington half but the home defence was quite equal to the attack and play settled about mid-field. Warrington played vigorously, and ultimately Burgess got possession and passed to Lawless, that player transferr- ing to Jones who notched a try near the corner. Ratcliffe made an abortive:attempt at goal. The home team continued to press and Hockenhall made a fine run, being grassed near the line. The visitors now forced play into the home terri- tory but a free kick relieved matters. Half time arrived with Warrington leading by three points to nil. Hcckenhall scorad from an opening by Burgess. Jones kicked a goal from a rather difficult angle. PLTICIIER RUESELL'S ACCOUNT FOR W/DNEI A." This match was played at Warrington, on Saturday: before only a meagre attendance. Playing with the wind in their favour during the first half, the general run of the game went in favour Of the visitors, whose forwards made several vigorous rushes which were ably checked by Brown. The Engineers' played a superb defensive game. Eventually the home team were penalised, and Taylor succeeded in plac- ing a goal. This proved to be the only tangible score during this half. With the elements in their favour, the Engineers soon made matters warm ha the vicinity of the Widnes goal. and the outcome of brilliant passing movements resulted in tries being scored, the final reading as follows : —Fletcher Russells, ;aloe points ; Whines "A," two points.

Monday evening witnessed the holding of the fourth public meeting which has been promoted in support of the agitation against Mutlicipal Trading by members of the Corporation, either direct or through the means of limited com- panies. Parr Hall was again well filled though the attendance was not quite so large as at the previous meeting. Mr James Dodds occupied the chair and he was supported on the platform by Mr Norbury Williams, of Manchester, Mr EL Flem- ing (secretary ot the Non-Municipal Trading Com- mittee), Mr John Pemberton, Mr Septimus Kenyon, Mr William Dooley,Mr A Hulme, Mr S Gough and others. THE CHAIRMAN AND "A CONSPIRACY OF SILENCE." THE WJOD-BLOCK TENDERS. Mr. DODDS delivered hiS opening address as follows: —The members of your committee have invited you to meet them in order teat they may report to you the I progress which has been made since our last great and memorable gathering held between three and four weeks ago, and in order that they may confer with you, and receive your mandate in regard Le our further and future operations. Oa my own behalf, and on behalf of the committee, let me in the first place thank you for having milled around us once more on an incon- venient night and after an short an interval, in such large nunabere. There is no sign here of that" apathy if not revulsion," which, according to, shall I say, an inspired paragraph in Saturday's issue of a local paper professing Radical sentiments, "has come over many people since the last meeting ? " The wish is father to the thought. In any case lam mere than ever satis- fied, and I know that you, the property-owners and ratepayers of Warrington, are also more convinced than ever that the half has not yet been told in regard to the scandalous condition into which THE AIANA4EMENT OF THE MUNICIPAL AFFAIRS Of Warrington has been brought, and especially in regard to the trafficking innell directions and under various potexts with the Corporation by many members of the Council, all of it improper and immoral and injurious to the public well-being ; some of it in direct contravention offthe law, and the rest of it in direct con- travention of the spirit and the clear intention of the law. As you know tne prescribed notice of the resolution for an inquiry had not been given by a member of the Council so that nothing was done at the last meeting, and my letter was referred to the General Purposes Committee. MR. PEMBERTON'S SPEECH. THE RESOLUTION.

Mr. JOHN PENBEHTON moved the following resolution: " Three large public meetings of ratepayers and pro- perty owners of the Borough of Warrington having passed a resolution that application should ha made to the Local Government Board for a public inquiry to be held to investigate all Corporation contracts during the last five years. and several Councillors having express!cl themselves in favour of a thorough investigation by public inquiry, this meeting respectfully requests the Corporation to make application to the Local Govern- ment Board to direct such an inquiry." In referriag to the

against the "No-trading Committee" and the large number of people In the town which the committee represented. They hoped it was not so, and they trusted that they would see in the affairs of the Council daring the next twelve months a much needed improve- ment. (Hear,hear.) He did not think they need resent that expression too much, for after all the Chairman of the meeting was a " certain" man. (Laughter.) He was "certain" of the justice of his cause, and he was " certain "that he had the people of Warrington behind him. (Hear, hear, and applause.) They had a little evidence that they were getting on,and he thought they could have some more of a different kind. There were worse places on the face of the dountry than Warrington, (Hear,hear.) At their last meeting Mr Norbury Williams read a cutting from a paper, put into his hands at the commencement of the meeting, which told them that at Darwen the auditor—who was what an auditor should be—had directly compelled a councillor to resign. They thought that was

A FINE OBJECT LESSON TO WARRINGTON,

but it seemed that there was a sequel to the story. That councillor had presented himself tor re-election and was returned by a large majority. (" Oh !') He thought they might, however, take heart in Warrington. They might have been apathetic in the past, but they were not green enough to do that sort of thing. (Applause.) When once they had got the " old man of the seas off their shoulders" they were going to show that when Mr Norbury Williams wanted a black spot to point out in the future he must pass them and look at Darwen. (Hoar, hear.) He was not sure also wh Aher they could not give points to Blackburn. There they had the ad- vantage in possessing a councillor who could move a sweeping resolution in such terms as the following : " That we do not allow any councillor or alderman or official of this Council to hold shares or have any monetary interest in any firm or company doing business or likely to do business with the Cor- poration, it being distinctly understood that this resolu- tion shall not apply to railways or telThone companies.' That resolution had been duly seconded, but, like the Warrington Council, the Blackburn Council were con- tent to rest on the letter of the law. So far the two towns were pretty equal, but when it came to the feel- ing shown then they must decide,he thought, that they had the healthier and stronger public opinion, and 8) they could, he thought, claim to be ahead of Blackburn. (Hear, hear.) That was why he said they were getting on. Whilst the work might, so far, have fallen upon the shoulders of the few and might in a sense, continue , to do so, there was just as much call for patience and perseverance upon the people of Warrington as in the I members of the "No Trading Committee." Whilst a few men might do a great deal,still they (the ratepayers) were the only force in Warrington who could do any- thing with improper trading, and it was with them that the offenders in the Council were now fighting. Mr Pembertoa had referred to the strange silence of many of the members of the Connell. They realized that resentment died down in time. It was the same as in some of the stages of the South African war in which long tiring marches and ceaseless searching for a mobile enemy wore out the troops. It was the policy of the offenders in the Council to tire the ratepayers— to tire them down, lie thought they were going to disappoint them. They told the rate- parrs plainly enough that they could not see for the life of them THE IMPROPRIETY OF THEIR WAY OF TRADING with the Corporation. But what on earth had that to do with it ? The ratepayers could 539 the impropriety, and the Corporation were the servants of the ratepayers in this matter. It was for them to rise to the moral level of the ratepayers and not to he low in semi-dark- ness. No doubt a wrong impression has got abroad in regard to your meaning, and if you would like me to explain the matter to the pubiic meet- ing to be held on Monday night next, I shalt be pleased to do so and in such a way as would, lam sure, meet with your entire anoroval. Your ( obedient servant. (Signed) JAMES D-A)133." Since I was last in Warrington and sp Ate on this question I have been amused to find in one of your newspapers, I have for- gotten which—(laughter)—a complaint, which almost amounts to a whine, that we were daring to hold another meeting so soon. Mr Hulme is quite right when he says that these particular people, the 36, the three dczen who assemble in yonder Town Hal seem to manage the ratepayers and our affairs as well, and he was quite right when he said that these gentlemen ev;dently relied upon our public spirit and enthusiasm dying down. I want to say to you that it is perfectly tsue that in a single sentence at the last meeting I said that I did not think another big meeting would be necessary before next September. I want you to understand that we are going to hold meetings pretty regularly and continually until the wretched condition of affairs now prevailing in Warrington is put an end to. I hope the Corporation will not delude themselves in a fool's paradise by think- ing that by lying low and saying nothing that we shall die off, and become a mere memory, a mere chimera of the past. (Applause.) We intend to hold these meetings and to carry the reforms we are aiming at, and I hope that on the first March you intend to see that a man is elected elective auditor who will have two eyes in his head and who will declare plainly anything that is wrong that is going on. We' shall have our eyes open wide next October and give very short shrift to any of the nine men whose term comes to an end,and replace those who have done wrongly and put in their places men of our own. (Applause). rho Right Hon. James Bryce, not long ago, in a most glowing and magnificent oration in reference to municipal government, gave as tie key note of his address "Beware of small beginnings." Through his knowledge of affairs in the United States, and as the result of his experience in many countries of Europe and other continents, Mr Bryce has seen haw wrong doing and crooked doing on the parr of Corporations and public bodies seems always to seen, as mane other of these things start, from small beginnings. The present state of things began in Warrington many years ago, and your Corporation has got

JUST AIME' AS BAD AS A CORPORATION CAN GET. Although it has had its s nail beeinnings it has got to a state when the inert and women of Warrington ought to step in and say that the end is to be out to whae has been very well called " the intolerable vanity of elected persons," You have some of the !nest extraordinarily intolerably vain people in that Town Hall I have ever had to do with. (Laughter.) Allusion had been made to "a certain" Mr. Dodds. When I saw that I said, "I am glad, at any rate, there Is one man in Warrington who is certain ; for I have found very few who are." We want men in the Town Council to be in a similar way certain and then we shall be able, to rely upon them. Mr certain, has been asking me whether or not the towns of Darwen andl Blackburn may not be said to be even worse than War- rington. No, I don't think they are. I don't think the men of Darwen, peculiar as they are. or Blackburn, be- nighted as they are, would ever have stood in 1901 that their representatives in the Town Council should have cast them into A NETWORE, A WEB OF LIMITED COMPANIES, as you have them at Warrington. Don's go away with any Pharisaic feeling of purity with reference to these two towns, and think that you are holier than they are, because you are not, and I think you will have a big job before you have cleansed the town. It will take you all your time to see things right here. There was one sentence I employed when I last delivered a speech in Warrington—that you should clearly set you minds against this trading with limited companies. If you could convey that to the minds of your councillors and alderman as you desire you might easily attain the end in view, and if the Mayor and councillors could feel that you were determined, the nine men who come out for election on the 13t November next would feel it also, and as a consequence your victory would be practically safe. I should like the meeting to be very clear in this matter—that they will no longer tolerate this moot immoral system of trading through these devious paths with limited companies in which any of their coun- cillors are interested. Mr Pembe.rten in the course of his observations said that any of the gentlemen at the Town Hall could trade for 111 months, can commit illegal trading during that period, and that you cannot bring them to bock. I have endeavoured in previous addresses here to bring home to you the fact that THE MINITTE3 OF THE CORPORATION awl of the committees are open to your inspection if you are burgesses. Put down your shilling and demand to see not merely the minutes of your last Council , meetings but the minutes of every committee meeting ' held during the month, and by that means you could I have seen every order given out. I want that to be clear so that you need not go on for 111 months if an order ha a been given " A. 8.," because if it is given to- day you can see the register and record of it in the minutes the day after the meeting. Some of you should go from month to month conscientiously through all the minutes, for the law has given you this privilege,which none of you should forfeit and you could quickly put an end to trading with limited companies, If one of the committees bad to-day resolved to give an order to the Slate Company it need not go six months without being known:lt can be known within a month And it ought to be, and the whole of Warrington ought to protest in a month, and it must do so. That is my plan for War- rington. You must stand no nonsense when you get to the Town Hall. The law is clear. What is the name of your Town Clerk ? Mr Whittle ; Lyon Whittle. Well, even "lions " have to submit to the law, and if he or any other official having the custody of the hooks re- fuse; to allow you to see the main minutes, or the minutes of any committee, he can be summoned to a Court of summary jurisdiction and be fined ire a sum not exceediog LS. THE DUTIES AND FUNCTIONS OF A SCHOOL HDARD. ===== 0002601_19011220_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR BENNETT ON

"IDEALS."

The Criticism of the Corporation.

At the meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. SoCiety on Sun- day afternoon, the chair was taken by Mr William Beswick, who spoke upon " Labour and Progress." He advised the youog moo of to-jay to make the most ot their opportunities so that they might be fully equipped for the keen commercial struggle for exist- ence.—The speaker was Councillor Arthur Bennett. He was very freqeently accused, he said, of being an Waist, and he was very proud of that designation. He wished to speak that afternoon on the ideal man and the ideal town. In order to attain ideal manhood he pointed out that it was necessary to strive after physical and intellectual greatness, and to mould our lives according to the pattern of Christ. Physical exercise was necessary to develop the body, but he was inclined to think that the present craze for athletics was not a sensible one. He was an enthus:- astic footballer himself at one time, and he considered football one of the finest games, but he objected to the absolute worship of football. If the thousands who watched the football matches would engage in some healthy exercise themselves they would be doing some- thing towards helping the race to attain to physical perfection. Not only was it necessary for them to develop their muscles, but they must CSD develop their minds. Whereas they would get five thousand at a football match they woull hardly get five dozen at a lecture. They ought to take as much iuterest in intellectual pur- suits as in athletics, and it was only when these two were running together in perfect harmony that they would become the ideal men he would like to see. They might be as strong as Hercules and as wise as Solomon, but unless they tried to build their lives and characters according to that of Christ they fell short of the true ideal they ought to aim at. It was difficult of attainment but it was better to have tried and failed to reach these high ideals thee never to have tried at all. Dealing with the second part of his subject—the ideal town—Councillor Bennett remarked that Shelley once wrote "Hell is a city much like London." He (Councillor Bennett) was afraid Mr Norbury Williams would be inclined to apply that ex- pression to Warrington and he was certain, in the pre- sent state of the public mind, many would be inclined I to cheer. Warrington was not quite so bad as that. Whatever Warrington might be or whatever the War- rington Towa Council might be, the fault was in the people themselves. The Town Council was the peoples creation- The town was what they made it I and if there were faults let them take steps ,to remedy them. He would ask them not to believe at the slanders which had beea bandied about lately respecting:the Corporation. Do not believe,he continued that every man connected :with it a rogue and a thief but look into your own hearts and see whether your lives are straight and upright and whether they will bear the light of investigation. They must begin at home. It was hopeless to reform a town or the world unless they began with the

individual. One of the great doctrines of Christianity was "Charity thinketh no evil "and that was a lesson which needed to be learnt at the present time. Although Warrington was not what Shelley thought of London,in spite of what had been said about it, Warrington WAS very far short of what it ought to be and of the ideal town he would like to see. His ideal was a healthy, beautiful, wholesome town where the people could de- velop their lives on the lines he had alluded to. He , deprecated the cry of low rates when so much was ne,eded to improve the town. Councillor Bennet pro- ceeded'to refer to the id.nal town mentioned in a book entitled " The Garden City " and stated that something of the kind bad been carried out on a small scale at Port Sunlight and Bourneville. lie urged that they in Warrington might do more to make the town beautiful and wholesome. In conclusion he said they might have a city as beautiful as could be imagined but unless the citizens were upright, straightforward, physically strong and sound it was a farce. Let them remember that they were not working for the moment but for eternity.

A solo was well sung by Mr Walter Clay, and the service shortly afterwards concluded.

BOARD SCHOOL ANNUAL CONCERT.

There were large and appreciative audiences at the Drill Hall,on Runcorn,Moradayand Wednesday, the occa- sions being the usual annual,winter :concerts in connec- tion with the scholars of the Board School. The concerts are greatly looked forward toby the inhabitants and this year substantial progress has been made under the head mastership of Mr J F Moulton. The inspector's report is of a most gratifying nature, " both discipline and instruction are very good," and testifies to the solid work being carried on. The following items connected with the Board School will be read with interest, and ' will, to a certain extent, account for the large amount of interest taken in the work of the boys. The number of boys on the rolls is 485 ; total attendanee year end- i rig 30th June,l9ol, 189,135 ; average daily attendance, 449 ; 'percentage attendance, 94 per cent ; Ceuraty Council Scholarship winners, 2; town 'scholarships 5 ; teachers, senior .department, Messrs J C Cragg, J Brown, A E Parr, W Vernon ; junior department, Mr F Deady and Misses E Stormount, E H Hough, L Wood, L Horsefield and J Burton. The hall had been very prettily decorated with scenery lent by Mr Caddick and the platform presented quite a novel appearance. The first portion of the programme was taken up with a burlesque operetta entitled " Cram ; pr the local Exam." This sketch was, on the whole, a very enter- taining one. It is to be feared hoWever,that a consider- able portion of the audience did not appreciate its nice- ties as well as it might have done had the listeners pro- vided themselves with copies of the words. It was only by following the sketch very narrowly in this manner that the merits of the composition could be fully understood. The sketch was a difficult and intricate one, but to the credit of those who took the various parts be it said that they acted their portions right nobly. Master J Wood took the part of the dominie with considerable success and imparting the necessary dignity to the character de- serves special mention for his admirable singing. Other characters were :—Jinks, E Lunt; Smithson, a model boy, H Goodier ; Fitz Jones, an idle boy, A Blinston: Brown and Green, confederates, E Scrag, E Barker ; ghost of ginger-beer, D Ferguson ; ghost of latin gram- mar, K Branch ; ghost of algebra, W Wiehlade. The peg on which the operetta hung was the relish of boys for study. The music and the words lent themselves to juvenile treatment the solos being very prettily Yen- dersd by everyone of the performers. A burst of applause followed the fall of the curtain. Perhaps the finest performance of the evening WAS the selection from "Julius Cesar " illustrating the quarrel between Brutus and Cassias. The characters were sustained by Misters R Booth and D Garvie The enunciation 'was most distinct and the dramatic. instinct shone very brightly in both performers. Mas- ters R Moulton. T Steele and H Roberts personified " Doctors Three " and;elicited quite an ovation from the au iience for their efforts.—Master Cox was deservedly encored for a capital rendering of "Buttons." The little fellow gave a spirited interpretation of the piece most creditably. "The Union Jack," a scenic sketch, acted by 2h junior boys contributed to some of the most en- joyable moments of the evening and pleased every one. Miss L Wood interpreted with fine effect the touching song " The old rustic bridge by the mill." The second portion of true programme was taken up with set ections by a juvenile troupe of " Christy Minstrels." The troupe had been most carefully trained and the melody and the time were all but 'perfect. The jokes, too, were quite up to date in quality,the repartee between bones and the interlocutor being bright and witty. The choruses, solos and stump speeches were also given with verve and expression. The following items were given : "In Ohio," " Now the task is plain before us," the troupe ; " Aunt Dinah's Umbrella," W Tuckey ; "Sweet Genevieve," J Wood : " The darkest looking darkey," E Barker ; Many years ago," W Wisladale ; " The laughing farmer," F Robinson ; " The little muff," F Hughes ; " Coon drum Major, H Geodier " Sleepy Jacob," E Lunt ; " Only a common onion," K Branch ; " Jog along Josie," " Stump speech," J Higgins. The tamboe, were Masters G Higgins, F Robinaon, D Garvie, and J ood; bones, Masters H Hensball, H Goodier, E Lunt, and %V Tuckey ; interlocutor, Master Reginald Booth, accompanist, Mr W Vernon. The per formers were prepared by Messrs W Vernon, A E Parr, J Brown, Misses L Wood, E El Hough, L Horsefield and J Barton. The general arrangements were admirably carried out by Mr J F Moulton, head master, and Miss Stormount,Messre. F Dandy and J C Cragg. Mr Byfield also rendered invaluable assistance. On the proposition of Messrs W Handley arid J Lititler a vote of thanks was passed to Mr Caddick, for lend ng the scenere ; Mr A Abel, for loan of tent ; Mr Miles, for cutting costumes ; and Mr F Hatton, for getting the performers prepared.

Improvement Wanted at Higher

===== 0002601_19011220_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
Runcorn;

Sir,—Does it ever strike our worthy Councillors or their officials that a widening of the bridge crossing the railway at the top of Picow-street is an absolute necess- ity. Here we have one of the most thickly populated districts in the town, and no cart way or road for vehicles from Greenway-road to the bottom of the Holloway, a distance of half a mile. And another thing I wish to point out. At the entrances to ths Picow- street Bridge a number of posts are placed, presumably to obstruct the passage of the humble wheelbarrow, but in reality a fruitful source of accidents and danger. The other evening, whilst proceeding up Picow-street with a lady, my companion, not noticing the posts owing to the darkness, collided with one of the stumps, which inflicted a nasty blow on the left side, knocking the lady quite sick for a few minutes. If the Higher Runcorn Brigade and Company had to cross this gloomy abyss we should have the stumps removed and an incandescent light placed at each end without delay.—Yours truly

-NOBBY FLUKE.

LATEST TELEGRAMS The Boers and Surrender.

Delarey's Attitude.

The special correspondent of the " Daily News,' telegraphing from Heidelberg, Transvaal. on Mon- day, says :

The feeling prevails here that the war will he over within two months. It is rumoured to-dey that Delarey wishes to surrender. A number of Boer prisoners who have beec brought into Standerton say that hostilities wia cease on December :22nd. This would appear to indicate that yesterday's mee tings wsre subject to adjournment for one week for the final decision.

PIG-SLAUGHTERING ON UNLICENSED PREMISES AT RUNCORN.

===== 0002601_19011220_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
Summonses Quashed.

At the Rencorn Petty Sessions on Monday, before Sir R. Brooke and other magistrates, Ann Cowap, Dased Pilgrim, George Hughes' rod Mary Price were sum- moned at the instance of the Urban District Council for having unlawfully slaughtered pigs in unlicensed pre- mises.—Mr Lake appeared for the Council and the &fen dants were represented by Mr Knowles.—Mr Lake, after quoting sections of the various acts under which. they proceeded, called Mr James Percival, Nuisance Inspector, who ttated with respect to the fret defendant, Ann Cowap, that he bad served her personally with a written notice to the effect that she would be proceeded against if she did not cease 1 slaughtering pigs on her premises. Witness went to the defendant's house on the 4th host and saw certain pigs dressed for food. He was informed that they were being sold to the Co-operative Stores.—Cross-examined by Mr Knowles.—He could not produce any paper or hand bill which advertised that the killing ef pigs on private premises had not to be carried on.—Mr Knowles then submitted that there was no case. The Council ought to have put in as evidence; a newspaper and a handbill telling the defendant to cease killing pigs.—The I Act expressly stated that this was necessary and as this had not been done there was nothing for their worships to do but dismiss the summons. The magistrates, without retiring,said they ;saw the force of the objection and the case was dismissed.—Mr Lake said be would therefore withdraw the other cases.—An appeal for advocate's fees from Mr Knowles was refused.

C 0 NT Y BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.— V —The Corporation are prepared to Let, from the 2nd February next, all that commodious and well-built HOUSE known as the Old Warps, within the borough, containing four entertaining rooms.six bedroome.bathroorn, w c, butler's pantry, &c, with the lawn, orchard, 2 stalled stable and two cosch-houses. saddle-room, &e, Together with the FARM- HOUSE adjoining, with the stables, sheds, and other out-buildings, and about 22 acres of LAND, including two acres of Twiggery and limited grazing at Victoria Park. The farmhouse contains living room and kitchen, with dairy and cellar, three bedrooms and two garrets. There is stabling for three horses,l4 loose boxes and sheds,and accommodation for 12 cows. Permission to Inspect the premises can be obtained on application at the Old Warps. Tenders for the yearly tenancy of the whole of the premises. or for the House and Farm separately, to be sent in (endorsed "Tender for Old Warps, or Farm,") on or before the 31st day of December instant. Dated this 14th day of December, 1901. J. LYON WHITTLE, Town Clerk. Town Hall, Warrington.
